\displaystyle\quad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\cdot\frac{1}{\prod_{k=m_{1}+1}^{m_{2}}(x_{2}^{(k)}-x_{1}^{(k)})}\cdot(-1)^{\sum_{k=m_{1}+1}^{m_{2}}i_{k}}\cdot f\big(x_{i_{1}}^{(1)},\dots,x_{i_{m_{2}}}^{(m_{2})},0,\dots,0\big)

\displaystyle\quad=\frac{1}{\prod_{k=1}^{m_{1}}(x_{3}^{(k)}-x_{1}^{(k)})}\cdot\sum_{\delta\in\{0,1\}^{m_{1}}}(-1)^{\sum_{k=1}^{m_{1}}\delta_{k}}\cdot\frac{1}{\prod_{k=1}^{m_{1}}(x_{3-\delta_{k}}^{(k)}-x_{2-\delta_{k}}^{(k)})}\cdot\frac{1}{\prod_{k=m_{1}+1}^{m_{2}}(x_{2}^{(k)}-x_{1}^{(k)})}

\displaystyle\quad\quad\cdot\sum_{i_{1}=2-\delta_{1}}^{3-\delta_{1}}\cdots\sum_{i_{m_{1}}=2-\delta_{m_{1}}}^{3-\delta_{m_{1}}}\sum_{i_{m_{1}+1}=1}^{2}\cdots\sum_{i_{m_{2}}=1}^{2}(-1)^{\sum_{k=1}^{m_{1}}(i_{k}+\delta_{k}-1)}\cdot(-1)^{\sum_{k=m_{1}+1}^{m_{2}}i_{k}}f\big(x_{i_{1}}^{(1)},\dots,x_{i_{m_{2}}}^{(m_{2})},0,\dots,0\big)

\displaystyle\quad=\frac{1}{\prod_{k=1}^{m_{1}}(x_{3}^{(k)}-x_{1}^{(k)})}\cdot\frac{1}{\prod_{k=m_{1}+1}^{m_{2}}(x_{2}^{(k)}-x_{1}^{(k)})}\cdot\sum_{\delta\in\{0,1\}^{m_{1}}}(-1)^{\sum_{k=1}^{m_{1}}\delta_{k}}\cdot\frac{1}{\prod_{k=1}^{m_{1}}(x_{3-\delta_{k}}^{(k)}-x_{2-\delta_{k}}^{(k)})}

\displaystyle\quad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\quad\cdot\triangle_{S_{\mathbf{p}}}f\bigg(\prod_{k=1}^{m_{1}}\big[x_{2-\delta_{k}}^{(k)},x_{3-\delta_{k}}^{(k)}\big]\times\prod_{k=m_{1}+1}^{m_{2}}\big[x_{1}^{(k)},x_{2}^{(k)}\big]\bigg)

\displaystyle\quad=-\frac{1}{\prod_{k=1}^{m_{1}}(x_{3}^{(k)}-x_{1}^{(k)})}\cdot\frac{1}{\prod_{k=m_{1}+1}^{m_{2}}(x_{2}^{(k)}-x_{1}^{(k)})}\cdot\sum_{\delta\in\{0,1\}^{m_{1}}}(-1)^{\sum_{k=1}^{m_{1}}\delta_{k}}\cdot\frac{1}{\prod_{k=1}^{m_{1}}(x_{3-\delta_{k}}^{(k)}-x_{2-\delta_{k}}^{(k)})}

\displaystyle\quad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\cdot\int_{x_{2-\delta_{1}}^{(1)}}^{x_{3-\delta_{1}}^{(1)}}\cdots\int_{x_{2-\delta_{m_{1}}}^{(m_{1})}}^{x_{3-\delta_{m_{1}}}^{(m_{1})}}\int_{x_{1}^{(m_{1}+1)}}^{x_{2}^{(m_{1}+1)}}\cdots\int_{x_{1}^{(m_{2})}}^{x_{2}^{(m_{2})}}g_{S_{\mathbf{p}}}(t_{1},\dots,t_{m_{2}})\,dt_{m_{2}}\cdots dt_{1}.

Also, since
g_{S_{\mathbf{p}}}
is entirely monotone, we have

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle\leq\int_{x_{2}^{(1)}}^{x_{3}^{(1)}}\int_{x_{1}^{(1)}}^{x_{2}^{(1)}}\cdots\int_{x_{2}^{(m_{1})}}^{x_{3}^{(m_{1})}}\int_{x_{1}^{(m_{1})}}^{x_{2}^{(m_{1})}}\int_{x_{1}^{(m_{1}+1)}}^{x_{2}^{(m_{1}+1)}}\cdots\int_{x_{1}^{(m_{2})}}^{x_{2}^{(m_{2})}}

\displaystyle\qquad\quad\prod_{k=1}^{m_{1}}(v_{k}-u_{k})\cdot\begin{bmatrix}u_{1},v_{1}&\hbox{\multirowsetup; \ $g_{S_{\mathbf{p}}}$ }\\
\vdots&\\
u_{m_{1}},v_{m_{1}}&\\
t_{m_{1}+1}&\\
\vdots&\\
t_{m_{2}}&\end{bmatrix}\,dt_{m_{2}}\cdots dt_{m_{1}+1}du_{m_{1}}dv_{m_{1}}\cdots du_{1}dv_{1}

\displaystyle=\prod_{k=1}^{m_{1}}\Big[\big(x_{3}^{(k)}-x_{2}^{(k)}\big)\big(x_{2}^{(k)}-x_{1}^{(k)}\big)\Big]\cdot\sum_{\delta\in\{0,1\}^{m_{1}}}(-1)^{\sum_{k=1}^{m_{1}}\delta_{k}}\cdot\frac{1}{\prod_{k=1}^{m_{1}}(x_{3-\delta_{k}}^{(k)}-x_{2-\delta_{k}}^{(k)})}

\displaystyle\quad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\cdot\int_{x_{2-\delta_{1}}^{(1)}}^{x_{3-\delta_{1}}^{(1)}}\cdots\int_{x_{2-\delta_{m_{1}}}^{(m_{1})}}^{x_{3-\delta_{m_{1}}}^{(m_{1})}}\int_{x_{1}^{(m_{1}+1)}}^{x_{2}^{(m_{1}+1)}}\cdots\int_{x_{1}^{(m_{2})}}^{x_{2}^{(m_{2})}}g_{S_{\mathbf{p}}}(t_{1},\dots,t_{m_{2}})\,dt_{m_{2}}\cdots dt_{1}.

Combining these results, we can show that the divided difference of order
\mathbf{p}
is nonpositive on
N^{(\mathbf{p})}=[0,1]^{m_{2}}\times\{0\}^{d-m_{2}}
when
\mathbf{p}=(2,\dots,2,1,\dots,1,0,\dots,0)
.
Through the same argument, we can prove that this is also true for every
\mathbf{p}\in\{0,1,2\}^{d}
with
\max_{k}p_{k}=2
.
Due to Lemma
2
, we can therefore conclude that
f\in{\mathcal{F}^{d}_{\text{TCP}}}
.

Step 2:
f\in{\mathcal{F}^{d,s}_{\text{TC}}}\Rightarrow
(
11
) and (
12
) hold for all
S\neq\emptyset
and (
13
) holds for all
S
with
|S|>s
.

We now show that
f
satisfies (
11
) and (
12
) (recall (
30
) and (
31
)) for all nonempty subsets
S\subseteq[d]
and (
13
) (recall (
32
)) for all subsets
S
with
|S|>s
.
It can be readily checked that (
13
) holds for all subsets
S
with
|S|>s
.
Also, for each subset
S\subseteq[d]
with
1\leq|S|\leq s
, since
g_{S}
is entirely monotone, we have that

\begin{bmatrix}0,t_{k},&k\in S&\hbox{\multirowsetup; \ $f$ }\\
0,&k\notin S&\end{bmatrix}=\frac{1}{\prod_{k\in S}t_{k}}\int_{\prod_{k\in S}(0,t_{k}]}g_{S}((s_{k},k\in S))\,d(s_{k},k\in S)\geq g_{S}((0,k\in S))

for every
t_{k}>0
for
k\in S
,
and that

\begin{bmatrix}t_{k},1,&k\in S&\hbox{\multirowsetup; \ $f$ }\\
0,&k\notin S&\end{bmatrix}=\frac{1}{\prod_{k\in S}(1-t_{k})}\int_{\prod_{k\in S}(t_{k},1]}g_{S}((s_{k},k\in S))\,d(s_{k},k\in S)\leq g_{S}((1,k\in S))

for every
t_{k}<1
for
k\in S
.
The conditions (
11
) and (
12
) follow directly from these results.

Step 3:
f\in{\mathcal{F}^{d}_{\text{TCP}}}
and (
11
), (
12
) hold for all
S\neq\emptyset

\Rightarrow f\in{\mathcal{F}^{d}_{\text{TC}}}
.

Next, we prove that, for every
f\in{\mathcal{F}^{d}_{\text{TCP}}}
satisfying (
11
) and (
12
) for all nonempty subsets
S\subseteq[d]
, it holds that
f\in{\mathcal{F}^{d}_{\text{TC}}}
.
Observe that

f(x_{1},\dots,x_{d})=f(0,\dots,0)+\sum_{\emptyset\neq S\subseteq[d]}\triangle_{S}f\bigg(\prod_{k\in S}[0,x_{k}]\bigg)

for every
(x_{1},\dots,x_{d})\in[0,1]^{d}
.
Hence, by Lemma
1
, it suffices to show that, for each nonempty subset
S
, there exists a function
g_{S}
satisfying the conditions of Lemma
1
such that

\triangle_{S}f\bigg(\prod_{k\in S}[0,x_{k}]\bigg)=-\int_{\prod_{k\in S}[0,x_{k}]}g_{S}((t_{k},k\in S))\,d(t_{k},k\in S)

(36)

for every
(x_{k},k\in S)\in[0,1]^{|S|}
.
Here, we prove this claim only for the case
S=[d]
, but our argument can be readily adapted to other nonempty subsets
S
of
[d]
.

Step 3-1:
\triangle f
is absolutely continuous.

Recall the notation
\triangle=\triangle_{[d]}
.
We first show that
\triangle f
is an absolutely continuous interval function on
[0,1]^{d}
.
To show this, we use the following lemma, which we prove in Appendix
C.4.3
.

Lemma 3
.

Suppose
f
is a real-valued function on
[0,1]^{d}
whose divided difference of order
\mathbf{p}=(p_{1},\dots,p_{d})
is nonpositive for
\mathbf{p}=(2,1,\dots,1),(1,2,1,\dots,1),\dots,(1,\dots,1,2)
.
Then, we have

\begin{bmatrix}x_{1}^{(1)},x_{2}^{(1)}&\\
\vdots&;\ f\\
x_{1}^{(d)},x_{2}^{(d)}&\end{bmatrix}\geq\begin{bmatrix}x_{1}^{(1)},x_{3}^{(1)}&\\
\vdots&;\ f\\
x_{1}^{(d)},x_{3}^{(d)}&\end{bmatrix}

if
0\leq x_{1}^{(k)}<x_{2}^{(k)}\leq x_{3}^{(k)}\leq 1
for
k=1,\dots,d
, and

\begin{bmatrix}x_{1}^{(1)},x_{3}^{(1)}&\\
\vdots&;\ f\\
x_{1}^{(d)},x_{3}^{(d)}&\end{bmatrix}\geq\begin{bmatrix}x_{2}^{(1)},x_{3}^{(1)}&\\
\vdots&;\ f\\
x_{2}^{(d)},x_{3}^{(d)}&\end{bmatrix}

if
0\leq x_{1}^{(k)}\leq x_{2}^{(k)}<x_{3}^{(k)}\leq 1
for
k=1,\dots,d
.
Also, it follows that

\begin{bmatrix}x_{1}^{(1)},x_{2}^{(1)}&\\
\vdots&;\ f\\
x_{1}^{(d)},x_{2}^{(d)}&\end{bmatrix}\geq\begin{bmatrix}y_{1}^{(1)},y_{2}^{(1)}&\\
\vdots&;\ f\\
y_{1}^{(d)},y_{2}^{(d)}&\end{bmatrix}

if
0\leq x_{1}^{(k)}\leq y_{1}^{(k)}\leq 1
and
0\leq x_{2}^{(k)}\leq y_{2}^{(k)}\leq 1
for
k=1,\dots,d
.

Along with the conditions (
11
) and (
12
), Lemma
3
implies that, for every
0\leq x_{k}^{(1)}<x_{k}^{(2)}\leq 1
,
k=1,\dots,d
,

\begin{split}&\bigg|\frac{1}{\prod_{k=1}^{d}(x_{k}^{(2)}-x_{k}^{(1)})}\cdot\triangle f\bigg(\prod_{k=1}^{d}\big[x_{k}^{(1)},x_{k}^{(2)}\big]\bigg)\bigg|=\bigg|\begin{bmatrix}x_{1}^{(1)},x_{2}^{(1)}&\\
\vdots&;\ f\\
x_{1}^{(d)},x_{2}^{(d)}&\end{bmatrix}\bigg|\\
&\qquad\quad\leq\max\Bigg(\bigg|\sup\bigg\{\begin{bmatrix}t_{1},1&\\
\vdots&;\ f\\
t_{d},1&\end{bmatrix}:t_{k}<1\bigg\}\bigg|,\ \bigg|\inf\bigg\{\begin{bmatrix}0,t_{1}&\\
\vdots&;\ f\\
0,t_{d}&\end{bmatrix}:t_{k}>0\bigg\}\bigg|\Bigg)<+\infty,\end{split}

(37)

from which the absolute continuity of
\triangle f
follows directly.
Therefore, by Theorem
6
,
D\triangle f
exists almost everywhere (w.r.t. the Lebesgue measure) on
[0,1]^{d}
, and we have

\triangle f\bigg(\prod_{k=1}^{d}[0,x_{k}]\bigg)=\int_{\prod_{k=1}^{d}[0,x_{k}]}D\triangle f(t_{1},\dots,t_{d})\,d(t_{1},\dots,t_{d})

(38)

for every
(x_{1},\dots,x_{d})\in[0,1]^{d}
.
Furthermore, using Lemma
3
again, we can show that

D\triangle f(x_{1},\dots,x_{d})\geq D\triangle f(x_{1}^{\prime},\dots,x_{d}^{\prime})

(39)

whenever
x_{k}\leq x_{k}^{\prime}
for
k=1,\dots,d
, and they exist.

Step 3-2: Definition of
g
.

Now, we define a function
g
on
[0,1]^{d}
as follows.
For
\mathbf{x}=(x_{1},\dots,x_{d})\in[0,1)^{d}
, let

g(x_{1},\dots,x_{d})=\inf\big\{-D\triangle f(s_{1},\dots,s_{d}):s_{k}>x_{k},k=1,\dots,d\text{ and }D\triangle f(s_{1},\dots,s_{d})\text{ exists}\big\}.

(40)

For
\mathbf{x}=(x_{1},\dots,x_{d})\in[0,1]^{d}\setminus[0,1)^{d}
, the value of
g
at
\mathbf{x}
is determined sequentially as follows:

\displaystyle g(1,x_{2},\dots,x_{d})=\lim\limits_{t_{1}\uparrow 1}g(t_{1},x_{2},\dots,x_{d})\ \text{ for }x_{2},\dots,x_{d}<1,

\displaystyle g(x_{1},1,x_{3},\dots,x_{d})=\lim\limits_{t_{2}\uparrow 1}g(x_{1},t_{2},x_{3},\dots,x_{d})\ \text{ for }x_{1},x_{3},\dots,x_{d}<1,\ \dots\ ,

\displaystyle g(x_{1},\dots,x_{d-1},1)=\lim\limits_{t_{d}\uparrow 1}g(x_{1},\dots,x_{d-1},t_{d})\ \text{ for }x_{1},\dots,x_{d-1}<1,

\displaystyle g(1,1,x_{3},\dots,x_{d})=\lim\limits_{t_{1}\uparrow 1}g(t_{1},1,x_{3},\dots,x_{d})=\lim\limits_{t_{2}\uparrow 1}g(1,t_{2},x_{3},\dots,x_{d})\ \text{ for }x_{3},\dots,x_{d}<1,\ \dots\ ,

\displaystyle g(x_{1},\dots,x_{d-2},1,1)=\lim\limits_{t_{d-1}\uparrow 1}g(x_{1},\dots,x_{d-2},t_{d-1},1)=\lim\limits_{t_{d}\uparrow 1}g(x_{1},\dots,x_{d-2},1,t_{d})\ \text{ for }x_{1},\dots,x_{d-2}<1,\ \dots\ ,

\displaystyle g(1,\dots,1)=\lim\limits_{t_{1}\uparrow 1}g(t_{1},1,\dots,1)=\cdots=\lim\limits_{t_{d}\uparrow 1}g(1,\dots,1,t_{d}).

If we prove that (a)
g
is well-defined, (b)
g
satisfies the conditions of Lemma
1
, and (c)
g=-D\triangle f
almost everywhere (w.r.t. the Lebesgue measure), then by (
38
), we can guarantee that
g
is the desired function for the claim (
36
) when
S=[d]
.

Step 3-3: Well-definedness of
g
.

We prove that
g
is well-defined at every point
\mathbf{x}=(x_{1},\dots,x_{d})\in[0,1]^{d}
by induction on
|\{k:x_{k}=1\}|
.
From (
37
) and (
40
), it follows that

\bigg|\sup_{(x_{1},\dots,x_{d})\in[0,1)^{d}}g(x_{1},\dots,x_{d})\bigg|<+\infty.

Moreover, by (
40
),
g(x_{1},\dots,x_{d})\leq g(x_{1}^{\prime},\dots,x_{d}^{\prime})
for every
x_{k}\leq x_{k}^{\prime}<1
,
k=1,\dots,d
.
These boundedness and monotonicity of
g
ensure that, for fixed
x_{2},\dots,x_{d}<1
, the limit
\lim_{t_{1}\uparrow 1}g(t_{1},x_{2},\dots,x_{d})
exists, which means that
g(1,x_{2},\dots,x_{d})
is well-defined.
Through the same argument, we can show that
g
is well-defined at every
\mathbf{x}\in[0,1]^{d}
with
|\{k:x_{k}=1\}|=1
.

We now assume that
g
is well-defined at every
\mathbf{x}\in[0,1]^{d}
with
|\{k:x_{k}=1\}|\leq m-1
and show that it is also well-defined at every
\mathbf{x}
with
|\{k:x_{k}=1\}|=m
.
Without loss of generality, assume
\mathbf{x}=(1,\dots,1,x_{m+1},\dots,x_{d})
, where
x_{m+1},\dots,x_{d}<1
.
To show that
g
is well-defined at
\mathbf{x}
, we need to verify that

\lim_{t_{1}\uparrow 1}g(t_{1},1,\dots,1,x_{m+1},\dots,x_{d}),\dots,\lim_{t_{m}\uparrow 1}g(1,\dots,1,t_{m},x_{m+1},\dots,x_{d})

exist and coincide.
To establish this, it suffices to prove that
\lim_{t_{m}\uparrow 1}g(1,\dots,1,t_{m},x_{m+1},\dots,x_{d})
exists and satisfies

\lim_{t_{m}\uparrow 1}g(1,\dots,1,t_{m},x_{m+1},\dots,x_{d})=\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}g\Big(1-\frac{1}{n},\dots,1-\frac{1}{n},x_{m+1},\dots,x_{d}\Big).

The existence of the limit follows from the boundedness of
g
and the monotonicity:

\displaystyle g(1,\dots,1,t_{m},x_{m+1},\dots,x_{d})=\lim_{t_{1}\uparrow 1}\cdots\lim_{t_{m-1}\uparrow 1}g(t_{1},\dots,t_{m-1},t_{m},x_{m+1},\dots,x_{d})

\displaystyle\qquad\leq\lim_{t_{1}\uparrow 1}\cdots\lim_{t_{m-1}\uparrow 1}g(t_{1},\dots,t_{m-1},t_{m}^{\prime},x_{m+1},\dots,x_{d})=g(1,\dots,1,t_{m}^{\prime},x_{m+1},\dots,x_{d})

for every
t_{m}<t_{m}^{\prime}<1
.
Next, note that

\displaystyle\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}g\Big(1-\frac{1}{n},\dots,1-\frac{1}{n},x_{m+1},\dots,x_{d}\Big)\leq\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}g\Big(1,1-\frac{1}{n},\dots,1-\frac{1}{n},x_{m+1},\dots,x_{d}\Big)

\displaystyle\qquad\leq\cdots\leq\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}g\Big(1,\dots,1,1-\frac{1}{n},x_{m+1},\dots,x_{d}\Big)=\lim_{t_{m}\uparrow 1}g(1,\dots,1,t_{m},x_{m+1},\dots,x_{d}).

Thus, it remains to prove that

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}g\Big(1-\frac{1}{n},\dots,1-\frac{1}{n},x_{m+1},\dots,x_{d}\Big)\geq\lim_{t_{m}\uparrow 1}g(1,\dots,1,t_{m},x_{m+1},\dots,x_{d}).

(41)

Fix
\epsilon>0
.
Then, there exists
s_{m}<1
such that

g(1,\dots,1,s_{m},x_{m+1},\dots,x_{d})>\lim_{t_{m}\uparrow 1}g(1,\dots,1,t_{m},x_{m+1},\dots,x_{d})-\frac{\epsilon}{m}.

Similarly, there exists
s_{m-1}<1
such that

\displaystyle g(1,\dots,1,s_{m-1},s_{m},x_{m+1},\dots,x_{d})

\displaystyle>g(1,\dots,1,1,s_{m},x_{m+1},\dots,x_{d})-\frac{\epsilon}{m}

\displaystyle>\lim_{t_{m}\uparrow 1}g(1,\dots,1,1,t_{m},x_{m+1},\dots,x_{d})-\frac{2\epsilon}{m}.

Repeating this argument, we can find
s_{1},\dots,s_{m}<1
for which

g(s_{1},\dots,s_{m},x_{m+1},\dots,x_{d})>\lim_{t_{m}\uparrow 1}g(1,\dots,1,t_{m},x_{m+1},\dots,x_{d})-\epsilon.

This implies that

\displaystyle\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}g\Big(1-\frac{1}{n},\dots,1-\frac{1}{n},x_{m+1},\dots,x_{d}\Big)

\displaystyle\geq g(s_{1},\dots,s_{m},x_{m+1},\dots,x_{d})

\displaystyle>\lim_{t_{m}\uparrow 1}g(1,\dots,1,t_{m},x_{m+1},\dots,x_{d})-\epsilon.

Since
\epsilon>0
can be arbitrarily small, this proves (
41
), and therefore
g
is well-defined at
\mathbf{x}
.
By induction, we can see that
g
is well-defined at every point in
[0,1]^{d}
.

Step 3-4: Entire monotonicity of
g
.

Next, we show that
g
is entirely monotone.
To prove this, we use the following lemma, which derives the nonpositivity of the divided difference of
D\triangle f
of order
\mathbf{p}=(p_{1},\dots,p_{d})
from the nonpositivity of the divided difference of
f
of order
(p_{1}+1,\dots,p_{d}+1)
for
\mathbf{p}\in\{0,1\}^{d}\setminus\{{\mathbf{0}}\}
.
The proof of Lemma
4
is deferred to Appendix
C.4.4
.

Lemma 4
.

Fix
\mathbf{p}=(p_{1},\dots,p_{d})\in\{0,1\}^{d}\setminus\{{\mathbf{0}}\}
.
Suppose
f
is a real-valued function on
[0,1]^{d}
whose divided difference of order
(p_{1}+1,\dots,p_{d}+1)
is nonpositive.
Then, for every
0\leq x_{1}^{(k)}<\cdots<x_{p_{k}+1}^{(k)}\leq 1
,
k=1,\dots,d
, we have

\begin{bmatrix}x_{1}^{(1)},\dots,x_{p_{1}+1}^{(1)}&\\
\vdots&;\ D\triangle f\\
x_{1}^{(d)},\dots,x_{p_{d}+1}^{(d)}&\end{bmatrix}\leq 0,

provided that
D\triangle f(x_{i_{1}}^{(1)},\dots,x_{i_{d}}^{(d)})
exists for every
i_{k}=1,\dots,p_{k}+1
,
k=1,\dots,d
.

Fix
\mathbf{p}=(p_{1},\dots,p_{d})\in\{0,1\}^{d}\setminus\{{\mathbf{0}}\}
.
We prove that the divided difference of
g
of order
\mathbf{p}
with respect to the points
(x^{(k)}_{i},1\leq i\leq p_{k}+1,1\leq k\leq d)
is nonnegative for every
0\leq x_{1}^{(k)}<\dots<x_{p_{k}+1}^{(k)}\leq 1
,
k=1,\dots,d
.
We first consider the case where
x_{p_{k}+1}^{(k)}<1
for all
k=1,\dots,d
.
Since
D\triangle f
exists almost everywhere, there exists a sequence of positive real vectors
\{(\delta_{n}^{(1)},\dots,\delta_{n}^{(d)}):n\in\mathbb{N}\}
such that
\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\delta_{n}^{(k)}=0
for each
k=1,\dots,d
, and
D\triangle f(x_{i_{1}}^{(1)}+\delta_{n}^{(1)},\dots,x_{i_{d}}^{(d)}+\delta_{n}^{(d)})
exists for every
i_{k}=1,\dots,p_{k}+1
,
k=1,\dots,d
and for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
.
From the monotonicity (
39
) of
D\triangle f
and the definition (
40
) of
g
, it follows that

g\big(x_{i_{1}}^{(1)},\dots,x_{i_{d}}^{(d)}\big)=-\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}D\triangle f\big(x_{i_{1}}^{(1)}+\delta_{n}^{(1)},\dots,x_{i_{d}}^{(d)}+\delta_{n}^{(d)}\big)

for every
i_{k}=1,\dots,p_{k}+1
,
k=1,\dots,d
.
This implies that

\begin{bmatrix}x_{1}^{(1)},\dots,x_{p_{1}+1}^{(1)}&\\
\vdots&;\ g\\
x_{1}^{(d)},\dots,x_{p_{d}+1}^{(d)}&\end{bmatrix}=-\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\begin{bmatrix}x_{1}^{(1)}+\delta_{n}^{(1)},\dots,x_{p_{1}+1}^{(1)}+\delta_{n}^{(1)}&\\
\vdots&;\ D\triangle f\\
x_{1}^{(d)}+\delta_{n}^{(d)},\dots,x_{p_{d}+1}^{(d)}+\delta_{n}^{(d)}&\end{bmatrix}.

Since
\max_{k}(p_{k}+1)=2
, the divided difference of
f
of order
(p_{1}+1,\dots,p_{d}+1)
is nonpositive.
By Lemma
4
, we thus have

\begin{bmatrix}x_{1}^{(1)}+\delta_{n}^{(1)},\dots,x_{p_{1}+1}^{(1)}+\delta_{n}^{(1)}&\\
\vdots&;\ D\triangle f\\
x_{1}^{(d)}+\delta_{n}^{(d)},\dots,x_{p_{d}+1}^{(d)}+\delta_{n}^{(d)}&\end{bmatrix}\leq 0

for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
.
This proves that the divided difference of
g
order
\mathbf{p}
with respect to the points
(x^{(k)}_{i},1\leq i\leq p_{k}+1,1\leq k\leq d)
is nonnegative.

Now, we consider the case where
x_{p_{k}+1}^{(k)}=1
for some
k\in\{1,\dots,d\}
.
For notational convenience, we assume that
x_{p_{k}+1}^{(k)}=1
for
k\leq m
and
x_{p_{k}+1}^{(k)}<1
for
k>m
.
Then, by the definition (
40
) of
g
and the result established in the previous paragraph, we have

\begin{bmatrix}x_{1}^{(1)},\dots,x_{p_{1}+1}^{(1)}&\\
\vdots&;\ g\\
x_{1}^{(d)},\dots,x_{p_{d}+1}^{(d)}&\end{bmatrix}=\lim_{t_{1}\uparrow 1}\cdots\lim_{t_{m}\uparrow 1}\begin{bmatrix}x_{1}^{(1)},\dots,x_{p_{1}}^{(1)},t_{1}&\hbox{\multirowsetup; \ $g$ }\\
\vdots&\\
x_{1}^{(m)},\dots,x_{p_{m}}^{(m)},t_{m}&\\
x_{1}^{(m+1)},\dots,x_{p_{m+1}+1}^{(m+1)}&\\
\vdots&\\
x_{1}^{(d)},\dots,x_{p_{d}+1}^{(d)}&\end{bmatrix}\geq 0,

which means that the divided difference of
g
order
\mathbf{p}
with respect to the points
(x^{(k)}_{i},1\leq i\leq p_{k}+1,1\leq k\leq d)
is nonnegative in this case as well.
Thus, we can conclude that
g
is entirely monotone.

Step 3-5: Coordinate-wise right and left continuity of
g
.

It is clear from the definition that
g
is coordinate-wise left-continuous at each point
(x_{k},k\in S)\in[0,1]^{|S|}\setminus[0,1)^{|S|}
with respect to all the
k^{\text{th}}
coordinates where
x_{k}=1
.
Hence, to show that
g
satisfies the conditions of Lemma
1
, it remains to verify the (coordinate-wise) right-continuity of
g
.
The right-continuity of
g
on
[0,1)^{d}
is also clear from the definition of
g
.
We prove that
g
is also right-continuous at every point
\mathbf{x}=(x_{1},\dots,x_{d})\in[0,1]^{d}\setminus[0,1)^{d}
by induction on
|\{k:x_{k}=1\}|
.

We assume that
g
is right-continuous at every
\mathbf{x}\in[0,1]^{d}\setminus[0,1)^{d}
with
|\{k:x_{k}=1\}|\leq m-1
and show that it is also right-continuous at every
\mathbf{x}\in[0,1]^{d}\setminus[0,1)^{d}
with
|\{k:x_{k}=1\}|=m
.
Without loss of generality, let
\mathbf{x}=(1,\dots,1,x_{m+1},\dots,x_{d})
, where
x_{m+1},\dots,x_{d}<1
.
Suppose, for contradiction,
g
is not right-continuous at
\mathbf{x}
.
Then, without loss of generality, we can assume that

g(1,\dots,1,x_{m+1},\dots,x_{d})<\lim_{t_{m+1}\downarrow x_{m+1}}g(1,\dots,1,t_{m+1},x_{m+2},\dots,x_{d}).

Let
\epsilon=\lim_{t_{m+1}\downarrow x_{m+1}}g(1,\dots,1,t_{m+1},x_{m+2},\dots,x_{d})-g(1,\dots,1,x_{m+1},\dots,x_{d})>0
and choose any
s_{m+1}>x_{m+1}
.
Since

g(1,\dots,1,1,s_{m+1},x_{m+2},\dots,x_{d})=\lim_{t_{m}\uparrow 1}g(1,\dots,1,t_{m},s_{m+1},x_{m+2},\dots,x_{d}),

there exists
s_{m}<1
such that

g(1,\dots,1,s_{m},s_{m+1},x_{m+2},\dots,x_{d})>g(1,\dots,1,1,s_{m+1},x_{m+2},\dots,x_{d})-\frac{\epsilon}{2}.

For every
t_{m+1}\in(x_{m+1},s_{m+1})
, the entire monotonicity of
g
implies that

\displaystyle g(1,\dots,1,s_{m},t_{m+1},x_{m+2},\dots,x_{d})

\displaystyle\geq g(1,\dots,1,1,t_{m+1},x_{m+2},\dots,x_{d})

\displaystyle+\big(g(1,\dots,1,s_{m},s_{m+1},

\displaystyle x_{m+2},\dots,x_{d})-g(1,\dots,1,1,s_{m+1},x_{m+2},\dots,x_{d})\big)

\displaystyle\geq g(1,\dots,1,1,t_{m+1},x_{m+2},\dots,x_{d})-\frac{\epsilon}{2}.

By the right-continuity of
g
at
(1,\dots,1,s_{m},x_{m+1},\dots,x_{d})
with respect to the
(m+1)^{\text{th}}
coordinate, we have

\displaystyle g(1,\dots,1,s_{m},x_{m+1},\dots,x_{d})=\lim_{t_{m+1}\downarrow x_{m+1}}g(1,\dots,1,s_{m},t_{m+1},x_{m+2},\dots,x_{d})

\displaystyle\qquad\geq\lim_{t_{m+1}\downarrow x_{m+1}}g(1,\dots,1,1,t_{m+1},x_{m+2},\dots,x_{d})-\frac{\epsilon}{2}=g(1,\dots,1,1,x_{m+1},x_{m+2},\dots,x_{d})+\frac{\epsilon}{2}

\displaystyle\qquad\geq g(1,\dots,1,s_{m},x_{m+1},\dots,x_{d})+\frac{\epsilon}{2},

which leads to contradiction.
Hence,
g
is right-continuous at
\mathbf{x}
.
By induction, we can conclude that
g
is right-continuous on the entire domain
[0,1]^{d}
.

Step 3-6:
g=-D\triangle f
almost everywhere.

Lastly, we show that
g=-D\triangle f
almost everywhere.
Consider the set

A=\big\{(x_{1},\dots,x_{d})\in[0,1)^{d}:D\triangle f(x_{1},\dots,x_{d})\text{ exists and }g(x_{1},\dots,x_{d})\neq-D\triangle f(x_{1},\dots,x_{d})\big\}.

It suffices to show that
m(A)=0
, where
m
denotes the Lebesgue measure.
First, observe that if
x_{k}<x_{k}^{\prime}
for
k=1,\dots,d
, and
D\triangle f(x_{1},\dots,x_{d})
,
D\triangle f(x_{1}^{\prime},\dots,x_{d}^{\prime})
exist, then by (
39
) and (
40
),

-D\triangle f(x_{1},\dots,x_{d})\leq g(x_{1},\dots,x_{d})\leq-D\triangle f(x_{1}^{\prime},\dots,x_{d}^{\prime})\leq g(x_{1}^{\prime},\dots,x_{d}^{\prime}).

This implies that, for each
\mathbf{c}=(c_{1},\dots,c_{d})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
, there exists an injective map from the set

A_{\mathbf{c}}:=\big\{u\in\mathbb{R}:u\cdot(1,\dots,1)+\mathbf{c}\in A\big\}

to
\mathbb{Q}
, the set of rational numbers,
from which it follows that
A_{\mathbf{c}}
is at most countable.
Let
\mathbf{1}=(1,\dots,1)
and let
\mathbf{e}_{k}=(0,\dots,0,1,0,\dots,0)
,
k=1,\dots,d
be the standard basis vectors of
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
.
Also, let
\mathit{P}
be a
d\times d
orthogonal matrix satisfying

\mathit{P}\Big(\frac{1}{\sqrt{d}}\cdot\mathbf{1}\Big)=\mathbf{e}_{1}

and let
\phi_{\mathit{P}}
be the corresponding linear map defined by
\phi_{\mathit{P}}(\mathbf{x})=\mathit{P}\mathbf{x}
for
\mathbf{x}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
.
Then, we can derive that

\displaystyle m(A)

\displaystyle=m(\phi(A))=\int\cdots\int 1\big\{(y_{1},\dots,y_{d})\in\phi_{\mathit{P}}(A)\big\}\,dy_{1}\cdots dy_{d}

\displaystyle=\int\cdots\int 1\bigg\{\sum_{k=1}^{d}y_{k}\mathbf{e}_{k}\in\phi_{\mathit{P}}(A)\bigg\}\,dy_{1}\cdots dy_{d}=\int\cdots\int 1\bigg\{\sum_{k=1}^{d}y_{k}\phi_{\mathit{P}}^{-1}(\mathbf{e}_{k})\in A\bigg\}\,dy_{1}\cdots dy_{d}

\displaystyle=\int\cdots\int 1\bigg\{\frac{y_{1}}{\sqrt{d}}\cdot\mathbf{1}+\sum_{k=2}^{d}y_{k}\phi_{\mathit{P}}^{-1}(\mathbf{e}_{k})\in A\bigg\}\,dy_{1}\cdots dy_{d}=\int\cdots\int 0\,dy_{2}\cdots dy_{d}=0,

where the second-to-last inequality is from the fact that

1\bigg\{y_{1}\in{\mathbb{R}}:\frac{y_{1}}{\sqrt{d}}\cdot\mathbf{1}+\sum_{k=2}^{d}y_{k}\phi_{\mathit{P}}^{-1}(\mathbf{e}_{k})\in A\bigg\}

is at most countable for fixed
y_{2},\dots,y_{d}\in{\mathbb{R}}
.

To sum up, we showed that
g
satisfies the conditions of Lemma
1
and
g=-D\triangle f
almost everywhere.
By (
38
), it follows that
g
is the desired function for the claim (
36
) when
S=[d]
.
Using similar arguments, we can find a function
g_{S}
for the claim (
36
) for each nonempty subset
S
.
Consequently, Lemma
1
proves that
f\in{\mathcal{F}^{d}_{\text{TC}}}
.

Step 4:
f\in{\mathcal{F}^{d}_{\text{TC}}}
and (
13
) holds for all
S
with
|S|>s\Rightarrow f\in{\mathcal{F}^{d,s}_{\text{TC}}}
.

We wrap up the proof by showing that if we further assume that
f
satisfies (
13
) for every subset
S
with
|S|>s
, then
f\in{\mathcal{F}^{d,s}_{\text{TC}}}
.
In this case, it follows directly that
\triangle_{S}f=0
for every subset
S
with
|S|>s
.
Thus, we can express
f
as

f(x_{1},\dots,x_{d})=f(0,\dots,0)+\sum_{\emptyset\neq S\subseteq[d]}\triangle_{S}f\bigg(\prod_{k\in S}[0,x_{k}]\bigg)=f(0,\dots,0)+\sum_{S:1\leq|S|\leq s}\triangle_{S}f\bigg(\prod_{k\in S}[0,x_{k}]\bigg)

for
(x_{1},\dots,x_{d})\in[0,1]^{d}
.
By using
g_{S}
satisfying the claim (
36
) and Lemma
1
again, we can therefore conclude that
f\in{\mathcal{F}^{d,s}_{\text{TC}}}
.
∎

C.1.2
Proof of Proposition
3

We use the following lemma in our proof of Proposition
3
.
This lemma can be proved via repeated applications of the fundamental theorem of calculus.
For details of the proof, we refer readers to
Ki et al. [34, Section 11.4.3]
.

Lemma 5

(Lemma 11.25 of
Ki et al. [34]
)
.

Suppose
g:[0,1]^{m}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}
is smooth in the sense that, for every
\mathbf{p}\in\{0,1\}^{m}
, it has a continuous derivative
g^{(\mathbf{p})}
on
[0,1]^{m}
.
For each
\mathbf{p}\in\{0,1\}^{m}
, let
S_{\mathbf{p}}=\{k\in\{1,\dots,m\}:p_{k}=1\}
and
{\mathbf{t}}^{(\mathbf{p})}=(t_{k},k\in S_{p})
.
Then,
g
can be expressed as

g(x_{1},\dots,x_{m})=g(0,\dots,0)+\sum_{\mathbf{p}\in\{0,1\}^{m}\setminus\{{\mathbf{0}}\}}\int_{\prod_{k\in S_{\mathbf{p}}}[0,x_{k}]}g^{(\mathbf{p})}(\widetilde{\mathbf{t}}^{(\mathbf{p})})\,d{\mathbf{t}}^{(\mathbf{p})}

for
x=(x_{1},\dots,x_{m})\in[0,1]^{m}
,
where
\widetilde{\mathbf{t}}^{(\mathbf{p})}
is an
m
-dimensional extension of
{\mathbf{t}}^{(\mathbf{p})}
where

\displaystyle\widetilde{t}^{(\mathbf{p})}_{k}=\begin{cases}t_{k}&\mbox{if }k\in S_{\mathbf{p}}\\
0&\mbox{otherwise}.\end{cases}

Proof of Proposition
3
.

Suppose
f^{(\mathbf{p})}
exists and is continuous on
[0,1]^{d}
for every
\mathbf{p}\in\{0,1,2\}^{d}
.
Assume further that
f^{(\mathbf{p})}=0
for all
\mathbf{p}\in\{0,1\}^{d}
with
\sum_{k}p_{k}>s
.
For each nonempty subset
S\subseteq[d]
, let
\mathbf{p}_{S}
denote the
d
-dimensional vector for which
(p_{S})_{k}=1
if
k\in S
, and
(p_{S})_{k}=0
otherwise.
Also, for each
S
, let
f_{S}
be the restriction of
f^{(\mathbf{p}_{S})}
to
\prod_{k\in S}[0,1]\times\prod_{k\notin S}\{0\}
.
Note that
f_{S}=0
for every
S
with
|S|>s
.
Since
f
is smooth in the sense of Lemma
5
, we can write it as

\displaystyle f(x_{1},\dots,x_{d})

\displaystyle=f(0,\dots,0)+\sum_{\emptyset\neq S\subseteq[d]}\int_{\prod_{k\in S}[0,x_{k}]}f_{S}((t_{k},k\in S))\,d(t_{k},k\in S)

\displaystyle=f(0,\dots,0)+\sum_{S:1\leq|S|\leq s}\int_{\prod_{k\in S}[0,x_{k}]}f_{S}((t_{k},k\in S))\,d(t_{k},k\in S)

for
(x_{1},\dots,x_{d})\in[0,1]^{d}
.
Also, since
f_{S}
are continuous, if
-f_{S}
are entirely monotone, then we can apply Lemma
1
to conclude that
f\in{\mathcal{F}^{d,s}_{\text{TC}}}
.

We now show that
-f_{S}
is entirely monotone for every
S
with
1\leq|S|\leq s
.
Without loss of generality, we verify this only for
S=[m]
.
Since
f_{S}
is smooth in the sense of Lemma
5
, it can be expressed as

\displaystyle f_{S}(x_{1},\dots,x_{m})

\displaystyle=f_{S}(0,\dots,0)+\sum_{\mathbf{p}\in\{0,1\}^{m}\setminus\{{\mathbf{0}}\}}\int_{\prod_{k\in S_{\mathbf{p}}}[0,x_{k}]}f_{S}^{(\mathbf{p})}(\widetilde{\mathbf{t}}^{(\mathbf{p})})\,d{\mathbf{t}}^{(\mathbf{p})}

for
(x_{1},\dots,x_{m})\in[0,1]^{m}
.
For each
\mathbf{l}=(l_{1},\dots,l_{m})\in\{0,1\}^{m}\setminus\{{\mathbf{0}}\}
, the divided difference of
f_{S}
of order
\mathbf{l}
is thus given by

\begin{bmatrix}x_{1}^{(1)},\dots,x_{l_{1}+1}^{(1)}&\\
\vdots&;\ f_{S}\\
x_{1}^{(m)},\dots,x_{l_{m}+1}^{(m)}&\end{bmatrix}=\sum_{\mathbf{p}:S_{\mathbf{l}}\subseteq S_{\mathbf{p}}}\int_{\prod_{k\in S_{\mathbf{l}}}(x_{1}^{(k)},x_{2}^{(k)}]\times\prod_{k\in S_{\mathbf{p}}\setminus S_{\mathbf{l}}}[0,x_{1}^{(k)}]}f_{S}^{(\mathbf{p})}(\widetilde{\mathbf{t}}^{(\mathbf{p})})\,d\mathbf{t}^{(p)}.

Note that
f_{S}^{(\mathbf{p})}
is the restriction of
f^{(p_{1}+1,\dots,p_{m}+1,0,\dots,0)}
to
[0,1]^{m}\times\{0\}^{d-m}
and that
\max_{k}(p_{k}+1)=2
.
Hence, by the assumption that
f^{(\mathbf{p})}\leq 0
for every
\mathbf{p}\in\{0,1,2\}^{d}
with
\max_{k}p_{k}=2
, we have
f_{S}^{(\mathbf{p})}\leq 0
for every
\mathbf{p}\in\{0,1\}^{m}\setminus\{{\mathbf{0}}\}
.
This ensures that the divided difference of
f_{S}
of order
\mathbf{l}
is nonpositive for every
\mathbf{l}\in\{0,1\}^{m}\setminus\{{\mathbf{0}}\}
, which means that
-f_{S}
is entirely monotone.
∎

C.2
Proofs of Propositions in Section
4

C.2.1
Proof of Proposition
4

Note that the least squares criterion only depends on the values of functions at the design points
\mathbf{x}^{(1)},\dots,\mathbf{x}^{(n)}
.
Proposition
4
is thus a direct consequence of the following lemma, which states that, for every function
f\in{\mathcal{F}^{d}_{\text{TCP}}}
satisfying (
13
) (recall (
32
)) for every subset
S
with
|S|>s
, we can always find a function in
{\mathcal{F}^{d,s}_{\text{TC}}}
that agrees with
f
at all
\mathbf{x}^{(1)},\dots,\mathbf{x}^{(n)}
.
In other words, there always exists an element of
{\mathcal{F}^{d,s}_{\text{TC}}}
that is indistinguishable from
f
at
\mathbf{x}^{(1)},\dots,\mathbf{x}^{(n)}
.

Lemma 6
.

Suppose
f\in{\mathcal{F}^{d}_{\text{TCP}}}
satisfies the interaction restriction condition (
13
) for every subset
S
of
[d]
with
|S|>s
.
Then, there exists
g\in{\mathcal{F}^{d,s}_{\text{TC}}}
such that
g(\mathbf{x}^{(i)})=f(\mathbf{x}^{(i)})
for all
i=1,\dots,n
.

Proof of Lemma
6
.

For each
k=1,\dots,d
, we write

\big\{0,x_{k}^{(1)},\dots,x_{k}^{(n)},1\big\}=\big\{u_{0}^{(k)},\dots,u_{n_{k}}^{(k)}\big\}

where
0=u_{0}^{(k)}<\cdots<u_{n_{k}}^{(k)}=1
.
As there can be duplications among
x_{k}^{(i)}
,
n_{k}
may be smaller than
n+1
.
Also, for each
S\subseteq[d]
with
1\leq|S|\leq s
, we let

\widebar{L}_{S}:=\bigg(\prod_{k\in S}\big\{u_{0}^{(k)},\dots,u_{n_{k}-1}^{(k)}\big\}\bigg)\setminus\{{\mathbf{0}}\}.

Note that
\widebar{L}_{S}\subseteq L_{S}
for each
S
.

We construct
f_{\beta,\nu}\in{\mathcal{F}^{d,s}_{\text{TC}}}
that agrees with
f
at the design points
{\mathbf{x}}^{(1)},\dots,{\mathbf{x}}^{(n)}
explicitly as follows.
First, for each
S\subseteq[d]
with
1\leq|S|\leq s
,
\mathbf{l}=(l_{k},k\in S)\in\prod_{k\in S}\{0,1,\dots,n_{k}-1\}
, and
k=1,\dots,d
, we let

p_{k}(S,\mathbf{l})=\begin{cases}0&\text{if }k\notin S\text{ or }l_{k}=0\\
l_{k}-1&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}\quad\text{ and }\quad q_{k}(S,\mathbf{l})=\begin{cases}0&\text{if }k\notin S\\
l_{k}+1&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

Observe that
0\leq q_{k}(S,\mathbf{l})-p_{k}(S,\mathbf{l})\leq 2
and that
q_{k}(S,\mathbf{l})-p_{k}(S,\mathbf{l})=2
if
k\in S
and
l_{k}\neq 0
.
It thus follows that
\max_{k}q_{k}(S,\mathbf{l})-p_{k}(S,\mathbf{l})=2
, unless
\mathbf{l}=(0,k\in S)
.
Next, we let
\beta_{0}=f(0,\dots,0)
and, for each
S
, let

\beta_{S}=\begin{bmatrix}u_{p_{1}(S,\mathbf{l})}^{(1)},\dots,u_{q_{1}(S,\mathbf{l})}^{(1)}&\\
\vdots&;\ f\\
u_{p_{d}(S,\mathbf{l})}^{(d)},\dots,u_{q_{d}(S,\mathbf{l})}^{(d)}&\end{bmatrix}.

(42)

Also, for each
S
, we let
\nu_{S}
be the discrete measure supported on
\widebar{L}_{S}
(
\subseteq L_{S}
) for which

\nu_{S}\big(\big\{\big(u_{l_{k}}^{(k)},k\in S\big)\big\}\big)=-\bigg(\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\in S\\
l_{k}\neq 0\end{subarray}}\big(u_{l_{k}+1}^{(k)}-u_{l_{k}-1}^{(k)}\big)\bigg)\cdot\begin{bmatrix}u_{p_{1}(S,\mathbf{l})}^{(1)},\dots,u_{q_{1}(S,\mathbf{l})}^{(1)}&\\
\vdots&;\ f\\
u_{p_{d}(S,\mathbf{l})}^{(d)},\dots,u_{q_{d}(S,\mathbf{l})}^{(d)}&\end{bmatrix}

(43)

for
\mathbf{l}=(l_{k},k\in S)\in\prod_{k\in S}\{0,1,\dots,n_{k}-1\}\setminus\{{\mathbf{0}}\}
.
Since
f\in{\mathcal{F}^{d}_{\text{TCP}}}
, we have

\begin{bmatrix}u_{p_{1}(S,\mathbf{l})}^{(1)},\dots,u_{q_{1}(S,\mathbf{l})}^{(1)}&\\
\vdots&;\ f\\
u_{p_{d}(S,\mathbf{l})}^{(d)},\dots,u_{q_{d}(S,\mathbf{l})}^{(d)}&\end{bmatrix}\leq 0

for every
\mathbf{l}\neq(0,k\in S)
, and this ensures that
\nu_{S}
is a measure for each
S
.
Now, we prove that
f_{\beta,\nu}\in{\mathcal{F}^{d,s}_{\text{TC}}}
, constructed from these
\beta_{0}
,
\beta_{S}
, and
\nu_{S}
, agrees with
f
at the design point
{\mathbf{x}}^{(1)},\dots,{\mathbf{x}}^{(n)}
.
In fact, we can prove a strong statement.
We can show that
f_{\beta,\nu}(u_{i_{1}}^{(1)},\dots,u_{i_{d}}^{(d)})=f(u_{i_{1}}^{(1)},\dots,u_{i_{d}}^{(d)})
for all
i_{k}\in\{0,\dots,n_{k}\}
,
k=1,\dots,d
.
For this, we use the following lemma, which will be proved right after this proof.
Here, for each
S
and
v_{k}\in[0,1]
for
k\notin S
, we denote by
f_{S}(\cdot;(v_{k},k\notin S))
the restriction of
f
to the subset
x_{k}=v_{k}
,
k\notin S
of
[0,1]^{d}
.
Also, recall the definition of
\triangle
from (
35
).

Lemma 7
.

Suppose we are given
S\subseteq[d]
and
v_{k}\in[0,1]
for each
k\notin S
.
Then, we have that

\displaystyle\triangle f_{S}\big(\cdot;(v_{k},k\notin S)\big)\bigg(\prod_{k\in S}\big[0,u_{i_{k}}^{(k)}\big]\bigg)

\displaystyle\quad=\sum_{\mathbf{l}\in\prod_{k\in S}\{0,\dots,n_{k}-1\}}\prod_{k\in S}\big(u_{i_{k}}^{(k)}-u_{l_{k}}^{(k)}\big)_{+}\cdot\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\in S\\
l_{k}\neq 0\end{subarray}}\big(u_{l_{k}+1}^{(k)}-u_{l_{k}-1}^{(k)}\big)\cdot\begin{bmatrix}u_{p_{k}(S,\mathbf{l})}^{(k)},\dots,u_{q_{k}(S,\mathbf{l})}^{(k)},&k\in S&\hbox{\multirowsetup; \ $f$ }\\
v_{k},&k\notin S&\end{bmatrix}

(44)

for every
i_{k}\in\{0,1,\dots,n_{k}\}
,
k=1,\dots,d
.

It follows from Lemma
7
that, for each
S
,

\displaystyle\beta_{S}\cdot\prod_{k\in S}u_{i_{k}}^{(k)}-\sum_{\mathbf{l}\in\prod_{k\in S}\{0,\dots,n_{k}-1\}\setminus\{{\mathbf{0}}\}}\bigg(\prod_{k\in S}\big(u_{i_{k}}^{(k)}-u_{l_{k}}^{(k)}\big)_{+}\bigg)\cdot\nu_{S}\big(\big\{\big(u_{l_{k}}^{(k)},k\in S\big)\big\}\big)

\displaystyle\quad=\sum_{\mathbf{l}\in\prod_{k\in S}\{0,\dots,n_{k}-1\}}\bigg(\prod_{k\in S}\big(u_{i_{k}}^{(k)}-u_{l_{k}}^{(k)}\big)_{+}\bigg)\cdot\bigg(\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\in S\\
l_{k}\neq 0\end{subarray}}\big(u_{l_{k}+1}^{(k)}-u_{l_{k}-1}^{(k)}\big)\bigg)\cdot\begin{bmatrix}u_{p_{k}(S,\mathbf{l})}^{(k)},\dots,u_{q_{k}(S,\mathbf{l})}^{(k)},&k\in S&\hbox{\multirowsetup; \ $f$ }\\
0,&k\notin S&\end{bmatrix}

\displaystyle\quad=\triangle f_{S}\big(\cdot;(0,k\notin S)\big)\bigg(\prod_{k\in S}\big[0,u_{i_{k}}^{(k)}\big]\bigg)=\triangle_{S}f\bigg(\prod_{k\in S}\big[0,u_{i_{k}}^{(k)}\big]\bigg)

for every
i_{k}\in\{0,1,\dots,n_{k}\}
,
k=1,\dots,d
.
Together with the interaction restriction condition (
13
) for
S\subseteq[d]
with
|S|>s
, this implies that

\displaystyle f_{\beta,\nu}\big(u_{i_{1}}^{(1)},\dots,u_{i_{d}}^{(d)}\big)

\displaystyle=f(0,\dots,0)+\sum_{S:1\leq|S|\leq s}\beta_{S}\prod_{k\in S}u_{i_{k}}^{(k)}

\displaystyle\qquad-\sum_{S:1\leq|S|\leq s}\sum_{\mathbf{l}\in\prod_{k\in S}\{0,\dots,n_{k}-1\}\setminus\{{\mathbf{0}}\}}\bigg(\prod_{k\in S}\big(u_{i_{k}}^{(k)}-u_{l_{k}}^{(k)}\big)_{+}\bigg)\cdot\nu_{S}\big(\big\{\big(u_{l_{k}}^{(k)},k\in S\big)\big\}\big)

\displaystyle=f(0,\dots,0)+\sum_{S:1\leq|S|\leq s}\triangle_{S}f\bigg(\prod_{k\in S}\big[0,u_{i_{k}}^{(k)}\big]\bigg)

\displaystyle=f(0,\dots,0)+\sum_{\emptyset\neq S\subseteq[d]}\triangle_{S}f\bigg(\prod_{k\in S}\big[0,u_{i_{k}}^{(k)}\big]\bigg)=f\big(u_{i_{1}}^{(1)},\dots,u_{i_{d}}^{(d)}\big)

for every
i_{k}\in\{0,1,\dots,n_{k}\}
,
k=1,\dots,d
.
∎

Proof of Lemma
7
.

We prove the lemma by induction on
|S|
.
We first consider the case
|S|=1
.
In this case, the claim of the lemma can be readily checked as follows.
Without loss of generality, here, we assume that
S=\{1\}
.

(
44
)

\displaystyle=u_{i_{1}}^{(1)}\cdot\begin{bmatrix}u_{0}^{(1)},u_{1}^{(1)}&\hbox{\multirowsetup; \ $f$ }\\
v_{2}&\\
\vdots&\\
v_{d}&\end{bmatrix}+\sum_{l_{1}=1}^{i_{1}-1}\big(u_{i_{1}}^{(1)}-u_{l_{1}}^{(1)}\big)\cdot\big(u_{l_{1}+1}^{(1)}-u_{l_{1}-1}^{(1)}\big)\cdot\begin{bmatrix}u_{l_{1}-1}^{(1)},u_{l_{1}}^{(1)},u_{l_{1}+1}^{(1)}&\hbox{\multirowsetup; \ $f$ }\\
v_{2}&\\
\vdots&\\
v_{d}&\end{bmatrix}

\displaystyle=u_{i_{1}}^{(1)}\cdot\begin{bmatrix}u_{0}^{(1)},u_{1}^{(1)}&\hbox{\multirowsetup; \ $f$ }\\
v_{2}&\\
\vdots&\\
v_{d}&\end{bmatrix}+\sum_{l_{1}=1}^{i_{1}-1}\big(u_{i_{1}}^{(1)}-u_{l_{1}}^{(1)}\big)\cdot\Bigg[\cdot\begin{bmatrix}u_{l_{1}}^{(1)},u_{l_{1}+1}^{(1)}&\hbox{\multirowsetup; \ $f$ }\\
v_{2}&\\
\vdots&\\
v_{d}&\end{bmatrix}-\begin{bmatrix}u_{l_{1}-1}^{(1)},u_{l_{1}}^{(1)}&\hbox{\multirowsetup; \ $f$ }\\
v_{2}&\\
\vdots&\\
v_{d}&\end{bmatrix}\Bigg]

\displaystyle=\sum_{l_{1}=1}^{i_{1}}\big(u_{l_{1}}^{(1)}-u_{l_{1}-1}^{(1)}\big)\begin{bmatrix}u_{l_{1}-1}^{(1)},u_{l_{1}}^{(1)}&\hbox{\multirowsetup; \ $f$ }\\
v_{2}&\\
\vdots&\\
v_{d}&\end{bmatrix}

\displaystyle=f\big(u_{i_{1}}^{(1)},v_{2},\dots,v_{d}\big)-f(0,v_{2},\dots,v_{d})=\triangle f_{\{1\}}\big(\cdot;(v_{2},\dots,v_{d})\big)\big(\big[0,u_{i_{1}}^{(1)}\big]\big).

Next, we suppose that the the claim holds for all
S\subseteq[d]
with
|S|=m-1
and prove the claim for subsets
S
with
|S|=m
.
For notational convenience, here, we only consider the case
S=[m]
, but the same argument applies to every subset
S
with
|S|=m
.
Through the same computation as in the case
S=\{1\}
, we can show that

(
44
)

\displaystyle=\sum_{l_{1}=0}^{n_{1}-1}\cdots\sum_{l_{m-1}=0}^{n_{m-1}-1}\prod_{k=1}^{m-1}\big(u_{i_{k}}^{(k)}-u_{l_{k}}^{(k)}\big)_{+}\cdot\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\in\{1,\dots,m-1\}\\
l_{k}\neq 0\end{subarray}}\big(u_{l_{k}+1}^{(k)}-u_{l_{k}-1}^{(k)}\big)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\cdot\Bigg[u_{i_{m}}^{(m)}\cdot\begin{bmatrix}u_{p_{1}(S,\mathbf{l})}^{(1)},\dots,u_{q_{1}(S,\mathbf{l})}^{(1)}&\\
\vdots&\\
u_{p_{m-1}(S,\mathbf{l})}^{(m-1)},\dots,u_{q_{m-1}(S,\mathbf{l})}^{(m-1)}&\\
u_{0}^{(m)},u_{1}^{(m)}&;\ f\\
v_{m+1}&\\
\vdots&\\
v_{d}&\end{bmatrix}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\quad+\sum_{l_{m}=1}^{i_{m}-1}\big(u_{i_{m}}^{(m)}-u_{l_{m}}^{(m)}\big)\cdot\big(u_{l_{m}+1}^{(m)}-u_{l_{m}-1}^{(m)}\big)\cdot\begin{bmatrix}u_{p_{1}(S,\mathbf{l})}^{(1)},\dots,u_{q_{1}(S,\mathbf{l})}^{(1)}&\\
\vdots&\\
u_{p_{m-1}(S,\mathbf{l})}^{(m-1)},\dots,u_{q_{m-1}(S,\mathbf{l})}^{(m-1)}&\\
u_{l_{m}-1}^{(m)},u_{l_{m}}^{(m)},u_{l_{m}+1}^{(m)}&;\ f\\
v_{m+1}&\\
\vdots&\\
v_{d}&\end{bmatrix}\Bigg]

\displaystyle=\sum_{l_{1}=0}^{n_{1}-1}\cdots\sum_{l_{m-1}=0}^{n_{m-1}-1}\prod_{k=1}^{m-1}\big(u_{i_{k}}^{(k)}-u_{l_{k}}^{(k)}\big)_{+}\cdot\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\in\{1,\dots,m-1\}\\
l_{k}\neq 0\end{subarray}}\big(u_{l_{k}+1}^{(k)}-u_{l_{k}-1}^{(k)}\big)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\cdot\Bigg[\begin{bmatrix}u_{p_{1}(S,\mathbf{l})}^{(1)},\dots,u_{q_{1}(S,\mathbf{l})}^{(1)}&\\
\vdots&\\
u_{p_{m-1}(S,\mathbf{l})}^{(m-1)},\dots,u_{q_{m-1}(S,\mathbf{l})}^{(m-1)}&\\
u_{i_{m}}^{(m)}&;\ f\\
v_{m+1}&\\
\vdots&\\
v_{d}&\end{bmatrix}-\begin{bmatrix}u_{p_{1}(S,\mathbf{l})}^{(1)},\dots,u_{q_{1}(S,\mathbf{l})}^{(1)}&\\
\vdots&\\
u_{p_{m-1}(S,\mathbf{l})}^{(m-1)},\dots,u_{q_{m-1}(S,\mathbf{l})}^{(m-1)}&\\
0&;\ f\\
v_{m+1}&\\
\vdots&\\
v_{d}&\end{bmatrix}\Bigg]

\displaystyle=\triangle f_{[m-1]}\big(\cdot;\big(u_{i_{m}}^{(m)},v_{m+1},\dots,v_{d}\big)\big)\bigg(\prod_{k=1}^{m-1}\big[0,u_{i_{k}}^{(k)}\big]\bigg)-\triangle f_{[m-1]}(\cdot;(0,v_{m+1},\dots,v_{d}))\bigg(\prod_{k=1}^{m-1}\big[0,u_{i_{k}}^{(k)}\big]\bigg)

\displaystyle=\triangle f_{[m]}\big(\cdot;(v_{m+1},\dots,v_{d})\big)\bigg(\prod_{k=1}^{m}\big[0,u_{i_{k}}^{(k)}\big]\bigg),

where the second-to-last equality is due to the induction hypothesis.
This proves the claim of the lemma for
S=[m]
and concludes the proof.
∎

C.2.2
Proof of Proposition
5

Proposition
5
directly follows from the lemma below, which states that, for every
f_{\beta,\nu}\in{\mathcal{F}^{d,s}_{\text{TC}}}
, there always exists
f_{\gamma,\mu}\in{\mathcal{F}^{d,s}_{\text{TC}}}
whose measures
\mu_{S}
are discrete and supported on
L_{S}
, and which coincides with
f_{\beta,\nu}
at all design points
{\mathbf{x}}^{(1)},\dots,{\mathbf{x}}^{(n)}
.

Lemma 8
.

Suppose we are given real numbers
\beta_{0}
,
\beta_{S}
,
1\leq|S|\leq s
, and finite measures
\nu_{S}
,
1\leq|S|\leq s
.
Then, there exist real numbers
\gamma_{0}
,
\gamma_{S}
,
1\leq|S|\leq s
, and discrete measures
\mu_{S}
supported on
L_{S}
,
1\leq|S|\leq s
such that

f_{\gamma,\mu}(\mathbf{x}^{(i)})=f_{\beta,\nu}(\mathbf{x}^{(i)})
for all
i=1,\dots,n
.

Proof of Lemma
8
.

Since
f_{\beta,\nu}\in{\mathcal{F}^{d,s}_{\text{TC}}}
, by Proposition
2
, we have
f_{\beta,\nu}\in{\mathcal{F}^{d}_{\text{TCP}}}
, and it satisfies the interaction restriction condition (
13
) for every subset
S
of
[d]
with
|S|>s
.
For such a function, in our proof of Lemma
6
, we constructed
f_{\gamma,\mu}
that agrees with it at the design points
{\mathbf{x}}^{(1)},\dots,{\mathbf{x}}^{(n)}
, with explicit choices of real numbers
\gamma_{0}
,
\gamma_{S}
, and discrete measures
\nu_{S}
supported on
L_{S}
.
Hence, Lemma
8
is a direct consequence of Proposition
2
and our proof of Lemma
6
.
∎

C.3
Proofs of Theorems in Section
6

In the proofs presented in this subsection,
C
will represent universal constants.
When a constant depends on specific variables, this dependence will be indicated with subscripts.
The value of a constant may vary from one line to another, even if the same notation is used.
Also, for simplicity, the exact values of constants are often left unspecified.

C.3.1
Simple Bound of
V_{\text{design}}(\cdot)

Here, in the following lemma, we present a simple upper bound of
V_{\text{design}}(f)
for
f\in{\mathcal{F}^{d}_{\text{TCP}}}
.

Lemma 9
.

If
f\in{\mathcal{F}^{d}_{\text{TCP}}}
, then

V_{\text{design}}(f)\leq\sum_{\emptyset\neq S\subseteq[d]}\bigg[\begin{bmatrix}0,1/n_{k},&k\in S&\hbox{\multirowsetup; \ $f$ }\\
0,&k\notin S&\end{bmatrix}-\begin{bmatrix}(n_{k}-1)/n_{k},1,&k\in S&\hbox{\multirowsetup; \ $f$ }\\
0,&k\notin S&\end{bmatrix}\bigg].

Proof of Lemma
9
.

In this proof, we reuse
f_{\beta,\nu}
that we constructed in our proof of Lemma
6
.
Recall our specific choices of
\beta_{0}
,
\beta_{S}
, and
\nu_{S}
(with
s=d
) from (
42
) and (
43
).
Note that, as

\{{\mathbf{x}}^{(1)},\dots,{\mathbf{x}}^{(n)}\}=\prod_{k=1}^{d}\Big\{0,\frac{1}{n_{k}},\dots,\frac{n_{k}-1}{n_{k}}\Big\},

here, we have

\big\{u_{0}^{(k)},\dots,u_{n_{k}}^{(k)}\big\}=\Big\{0,\frac{1}{n_{k}},\dots,\frac{n_{k}-1}{n_{k}},1\Big\}

for each
k=1,\dots,d
.
Since
f_{\beta,\nu}
and
f
have the same values at all design points
{\mathbf{x}}^{(1)},\dots,{\mathbf{x}}^{(n)}
, the definition of
V_{\text{design}}(\cdot)
implies that

V_{\text{design}}(f)\leq V(f_{\beta,\nu})=\sum_{\emptyset\neq S\subseteq[d]}\nu_{S}\big([0,1)^{|S|}\setminus\{{\mathbf{0}}\}\big).

Hence, it suffices to show that

\nu_{S}\big([0,1)^{|S|}\setminus\{{\mathbf{0}}\}\big)=\begin{bmatrix}0,1/n_{k},&k\in S&\hbox{\multirowsetup; \ $f$ }\\
0,&k\notin S&\end{bmatrix}-\begin{bmatrix}(n_{k}-1)/n_{k},1,&k\in S&\hbox{\multirowsetup; \ $f$ }\\
0,&k\notin S&\end{bmatrix}

(45)

for each nonempty subset
S\subseteq[d]
.
Here, we prove this equation only for
S=[m]
for notational convenience, but the other cases can also be proved exactly the same.
When
S=[m]
, our choice (
43
) of
\nu_{S}
leads to

\displaystyle\nu_{S}\big([0,1)^{|S|}\setminus\{{\mathbf{0}}\}\big)-\begin{bmatrix}0,1/n_{k},&k\in S&\hbox{\multirowsetup; \ $f$ }\\
0,&k\notin S&\end{bmatrix}

\displaystyle\ =-\sum_{l_{1}=0}^{n_{1}-1}\cdots\sum_{l_{m}=0}^{n_{m}-1}\bigg(\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\in\{1,\dots,m\}\\
l_{k}\neq 0\end{subarray}}\frac{2}{n_{k}}\bigg)\cdot\begin{bmatrix}p_{1}(S,\mathbf{l})/n_{1},\dots,q_{1}(S,\mathbf{l})/n_{1}&\hbox{\multirowsetup; \ $f$ }\\
\vdots&\\
p_{m}(S,\mathbf{l})/n_{m},\dots,q_{m}(S,\mathbf{l})/n_{m}&\\
0&\\
\vdots&\\
0&\end{bmatrix}

\displaystyle\ =-\sum_{l_{1}=0}^{n_{1}-1}\cdots\sum_{l_{m-1}=0}^{n_{m-1}-1}\bigg(\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\in\{1,\dots,m-1\}\\
l_{k}\neq 0\end{subarray}}\frac{2}{n_{k}}\bigg)\cdot\Bigg[\begin{bmatrix}p_{1}(S,\mathbf{l})/n_{1},\dots,q_{1}(S,\mathbf{l})/n_{1}&\\
\vdots&\\
p_{m-1}(S,\mathbf{l})/n_{m-1},\dots,q_{m-1}(S,\mathbf{l})/n_{m-1}&\\
0,1/n_{m}&;\ f\\
0&\\
\vdots&\\
0&\end{bmatrix}

\displaystyle\ \qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\quad+\sum_{l_{m}=1}^{n_{m}-1}\frac{2}{n_{m}}\cdot\begin{bmatrix}p_{1}(S,\mathbf{l})/n_{1},\dots,q_{1}(S,\mathbf{l})/n_{1}&\\
\vdots&\\
p_{m-1}(S,\mathbf{l})/n_{m-1},\dots,q_{m-1}(S,\mathbf{l})/n_{m-1}&\\
(l_{m}-1)/n_{m},l_{m}/n_{m},(l_{m}+1)/n_{m}&;\ f\\
0&\\
\vdots&\\
0&\end{bmatrix}\Bigg]

\displaystyle\ =-\sum_{l_{1}=0}^{n_{1}-1}\cdots\sum_{l_{m-1}=0}^{n_{m-1}-1}\bigg(\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\in\{1,\dots,m-1\}\\
l_{k}\neq 0\end{subarray}}\frac{2}{n_{k}}\bigg)\cdot\Bigg[\begin{bmatrix}p_{1}(S,\mathbf{l})/n_{1},\dots,q_{1}(S,\mathbf{l})/n_{1}&\\
\vdots&\\
p_{m-1}(S,\mathbf{l})/n_{m-1},\dots,q_{m-1}(S,\mathbf{l})/n_{m-1}&\\
0,1/n_{m}&;\ f\\
0&\\
\vdots&\\
0&\end{bmatrix}

\displaystyle+\sum_{l_{m}=1}^{n_{m}-1}\begin{bmatrix}p_{1}(S,\mathbf{l})/n_{1},\dots,q_{1}(S,\mathbf{l})/n_{1}&\\
\vdots&\\
p_{m-1}(S,\mathbf{l})/n_{m-1},\dots,q_{m-1}(S,\mathbf{l})/n_{m-1}&\\
l_{m}/n_{m},(l_{m}+1)/n_{m}&;\ f\\
0&\\
\vdots&\\
0&\end{bmatrix}-\begin{bmatrix}p_{1}(S,\mathbf{l})/n_{1},\dots,q_{1}(S,\mathbf{l})/n_{1}&\\
\vdots&\\
p_{m-1}(S,\mathbf{l})/n_{m-1},\dots,q_{m-1}(S,\mathbf{l})/n_{m-1}&\\
(l_{m}-1)/n_{m},l_{m}/n_{m}&;\ f\\
0&\\
\vdots&\\
0&\end{bmatrix}\Bigg]

\displaystyle\ =-\sum_{l_{1}=0}^{n_{1}-1}\cdots\sum_{l_{m-1}=0}^{n_{m-1}-1}\bigg(\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\in\{1,\dots,m-1\}\\
l_{k}\neq 0\end{subarray}}\frac{2}{n_{k}}\bigg)\cdot\begin{bmatrix}p_{1}(S,\mathbf{l})/n_{1},\dots,q_{1}(S,\mathbf{l})/n_{1}&\\
\vdots&\\
p_{m-1}(S,\mathbf{l})/n_{m-1},\dots,q_{m-1}(S,\mathbf{l})/n_{m-1}&\\
(n_{m}-1)/n_{m},1&;\ f\\
0&\\
\vdots&\\
0&\end{bmatrix}.

Repeating this argument with the
(m-1)^{\text{th}},\dots,1^{\text{st}}
coordinates in turn, we can obtain

\displaystyle\nu_{S}\big([0,1)^{|S|}\setminus\{{\mathbf{0}}\}\big)-\begin{bmatrix}0,1/n_{k},&k\in S&\hbox{\multirowsetup; \ $f$ }\\
0,&k\notin S&\end{bmatrix}

\displaystyle\qquad=-\begin{bmatrix}(n_{1}-1)/n_{1},1&\hbox{\multirowsetup; \ $f$ }\\
\vdots&\\
(n_{m}-1)/n_{m},1&\\
0&\\
\vdots&\\
0&\end{bmatrix}=-\begin{bmatrix}(n_{k}-1)/n_{k},1,&k\in S&\hbox{\multirowsetup; \ $f$ }\\
0,&k\notin S&\end{bmatrix},

which proves (
45
) for the case
S=[m]
.
∎

C.3.2
Proof of Theorem
1

The following theorem (Theorem
7
), established in
Chatterjee [13]
, plays a central role in our proof of Theorem
1
.
Before presenting the theorem, we briefly outline the problem setting considered in
[
13
]
.

Consider the model

y_{i}=\theta_{i}^{*}+\xi_{i}\quad\qquad\text{for $i=1,\dots,n$},

(46)

where
\mathbf{y}=(y_{1},\dots,y_{n})
is the observation vector,
\theta^{*}=(\theta^{*}_{1},\dots,\theta^{*}_{n})
is the unknown vector to be estimated, and
\xi=(\xi_{1},\dots,\xi_{n})
is a vector of Gaussian errors with
\xi_{i}\overset{\text{i.i.d.}}{\sim}N(0,\sigma^{2})
.
Also, let
K\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
be a closed convex set.
The least squares estimator of
\theta^{*}
over
K
based on the observations
y_{1},\dots,y_{n}
is then defined as

\hat{\theta}_{K}=\mathop{\rm argmin}_{\theta\in K}\|\mathbf{y}-\theta\|_{2},

(47)

where
\|\cdot\|_{2}
denotes the Euclidean norm.

Chatterjee [13]
studied the risk of this estimator
\hat{\theta}_{K}
, measured by the expected squared error
{\mathbb{E}}[\|\hat{\theta}_{K}-\theta^{*}\|_{2}^{2}]
, where the expectation is taken over the Gaussian errors
\xi_{i}
.
A key tool in their analysis is the function
h
defined as

h(t)={\mathbb{E}}\Big[\sup_{\theta\in K:\|\theta-\theta^{*}\|_{2}\leq t}\langle\xi,\theta-\theta^{*}\rangle\Big]-\frac{t^{2}}{2}

(48)

for
t\geq 0
.
If no
\theta\in K
satisfies
\|\theta-\theta^{*}\|_{2}\leq t
,
h(t)
is set to
-\infty
.

Chatterjee [13, Theorem 1]
proved that this function
h
has a unique maximizer, denoted by
t^{*}
.
Furthermore,
Chatterjee [13, Corollary 1.2]
, restated as Theorem
7
below, provides an upper bound on the expected squared error
{\mathbb{E}}[\|\hat{\theta}_{K}-\theta^{*}\|_{2}^{2}]
in terms of
t^{*}
.

Theorem 7

(Corollary 1.2 of
Chatterjee [13]
)
.

The expected squared error can be bounded as

{\mathbb{E}}\big[\|\hat{\theta}_{K}-\theta^{*}\|_{2}^{2}\big]\leq C(\sigma^{2}+{t^{*}}^{2})

for some universal constant
C
.

However, determining the maximizer
t^{*}
of the function
h
is often quite challenging.
Fortunately, we can utilize the following result in
[
13
]
to obtain an upper bound for
t^{*}
,
which can then be used instead to bound the expected squared error.

Proposition 6

(Proposition 1.3 of
Chatterjee [13]
)
.

If
h(t_{1})\geq h(t_{2})
for some
0\leq t_{1}<t_{2}
, then
t^{*}\leq t_{2}
.

Remark 1

(Well-specified case)
.

If
\theta^{*}\in K
, it holds that
h(0)=0
.
Thus, in this case, for every
t_{2}>0
with
h(t_{2})\leq 0
, we have
t^{*}\leq t_{2}
.

We now establish a connection between
\hat{f}_{\text{TC}}^{d,s}
and
\hat{\theta}_{K}
with an appropriate closed convex set
K
.
Consider the index set

I_{0}=\prod_{k=1}^{d}\{0,1,\dots,n_{k}-1\}.

We first re-index
{\mathbf{x}}^{(1)},\dots,{\mathbf{x}}^{(n)}
using
I_{0}
, so that
{\mathbf{x}}^{(\mathbf{i})}=(i_{1}/n_{1},\dots,i_{d}/n_{d})
for
\mathbf{i}=(i_{1},\dots,i_{d})\in I_{0}
.
Similarly, the observations
y_{1},\dots,y_{n}
are re-indexed as
(y_{\mathbf{i}},\mathbf{i}\in I_{0})
.
Next, define

T=\big\{(f({\mathbf{x}}^{(\mathbf{i})}),\mathbf{i}\in I_{0}):f\in{\mathcal{F}^{d,s}_{\text{TC}}}\big\}.

(49)

Note that
T
is a closed convex subset of
{\mathbb{R}}^{|I_{0}|}
.
Also, let
\hat{\theta}_{T}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{|I_{0}|}
denote the least squares estimator of
\theta^{*}
over
T
:

\hat{\theta}_{T}=\mathop{\rm argmin}_{\theta\in T}\|\mathbf{y}-\theta\|_{2},

where
\mathbf{y}=(y_{\mathbf{i}},\mathbf{i}\in I_{0})
.
Then, by the definition (
49
) of
T
, it is clear that the values of
\hat{f}_{\text{TC}}^{d,s}
at the design points are equal to the components of
\hat{\theta}_{T}
.
That is,

\hat{f}_{\text{TC}}^{d,s}\Big(\frac{i_{1}}{n_{1}},\dots,\frac{i_{d}}{n_{d}}\Big)=(\hat{\theta}_{T})_{\mathbf{i}}

for all
\mathbf{i}=(i_{1},\dots,i_{d})\in I_{0}
.
Consequently, the risk
R_{F}(\hat{f}_{\text{TC}}^{d,s},f^{*})
of
\hat{f}_{\text{TC}}^{d,s}
can be expressed in terms of the expected squared error of
\hat{\theta}_{T}
:

R_{F}(\hat{f}_{\text{TC}}^{d,s},f^{*})=\frac{1}{n}\cdot{\mathbb{E}}\big[\|\hat{\theta}_{T}-\theta^{*}\|_{2}^{2}\big],

where
\theta^{*}=(f^{*}({\mathbf{x}}^{(\mathbf{i})}),\mathbf{i}\in I_{0})
.
This relationship provides a way to analyze the risk
R_{F}(\hat{f}_{\text{TC}}^{d,s},f^{*})
of
\hat{f}_{\text{TC}}^{d,s}
by studying the expected squared error
{\mathbb{E}}[\|\hat{\theta}_{T}-\theta^{*}\|_{2}^{2}]
of
\hat{\theta}_{T}
.

We also employ the following concepts and notations in our proof of Theorem
1
.
For
\theta\in{\mathbb{R}}^{|I_{0}|}
and a vector of nonnegative integers
\mathbf{p}=(p_{1},\dots,p_{d})
, the discrete difference of
\theta
of order
\mathbf{p}
at
(i_{1},\dots,i_{d})
, where
(i_{1},\dots,i_{d})\in I_{0}
and
i_{k}\geq p_{k}
for
k=1,\dots,d
, is defined as

(D^{(\mathbf{p})}\theta)_{i_{1},\dots,i_{d}}=\frac{\prod_{k=1}^{d}p_{k}!}{\prod_{k=1}^{d}n_{k}^{p_{k}}}\cdot\begin{bmatrix}(i_{1}-p_{1})/n_{1},\dots,i_{1}/n_{1}&\\
\vdots&;\ f\\
(i_{d}-p_{d})/n_{d},\dots,i_{d}/n_{d}&\end{bmatrix},

(50)

where
f:[0,1]^{d}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}
is any function satisfying
f(j_{1}/n_{1},\dots,j_{d}/n_{d})=\theta_{\mathbf{j}}
for all
\mathbf{j}=(j_{1},\dots,j_{d})\in I_{0}
.
Since (
50
) only depends on the values of
f
at
(j_{1}/n_{1},\dots,j_{d}/n_{d})
for
\mathbf{j}=(j_{1},\dots,j_{d})\in I_{0}
, the discrete difference
(D^{(\mathbf{p})}\theta)_{i_{1},\dots,i_{d}}
is well-defined; it is independent of the choice of
f
.
These discrete differences
(D^{(\mathbf{p})}\theta)_{i_{1},\dots,i_{d}}
are consistent with the definitions provided in
Ki et al. [34, Section 9]
.

Example 1

(d = 2)
.

Let
\theta=(\theta_{\mathbf{i}},\mathbf{i}\in I_{0})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{|I_{0}|}
.
For
\mathbf{p}=(1,0)
:

(D^{(1,0)}\theta)_{i_{1},i_{2}}=\frac{1}{n_{1}}\begin{bmatrix}(i_{1}-1)/n_{1},i_{1}/n_{1}&\hbox{\multirowsetup; \ $f$ }\\
i_{2}/n_{2}&\end{bmatrix}=f\Big(\frac{i_{1}}{n_{1}},\frac{i_{2}}{n_{2}}\Big)-f\Big(\frac{i_{1}-1}{n_{1}},\frac{i_{2}}{n_{2}}\Big)=\theta_{i_{1},i_{2}}-\theta_{i_{1}-1,i_{2}}

provided that
i_{1}\geq 1
.
For
\mathbf{p}=(1,1)
:

\displaystyle(D^{(1,1)}\theta)_{i_{1},i_{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n_{1}n_{2}}\begin{bmatrix}(i_{1}-1)/n_{1},i_{1}/n_{1}&\hbox{\multirowsetup; \ $f$ }\\
(i_{2}-1)/n_{2},i_{2}/n_{2}&\end{bmatrix}

\displaystyle=f\Big(\frac{i_{1}}{n_{1}},\frac{i_{2}}{n_{2}}\Big)-f\Big(\frac{i_{1}-1}{n_{1}},\frac{i_{2}}{n_{2}}\Big)-f\Big(\frac{i_{1}}{n_{1}},\frac{i_{2}-1}{n_{2}}\Big)+f\Big(\frac{i_{1}-1}{n_{1}},\frac{i_{2}-1}{n_{2}}\Big)

\displaystyle=\theta_{i_{1},i_{2}}-\theta_{i_{1}-1,i_{2}}-\theta_{i_{1},i_{2}-1}+\theta_{i_{1}-1,i_{2}-1}

provided that
i_{1},i_{2}\geq 1
.
For
\mathbf{p}=(2,0)
:

\displaystyle(D^{(2,0)}\theta)_{i_{1},i_{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{n_{1}^{2}}\begin{bmatrix}(i_{1}-2)/n_{1},(i_{1}-1)/n_{1},i_{1}/n_{1}&\hbox{\multirowsetup; \ $f$ }\\
i_{2}/n_{2}&\end{bmatrix}

\displaystyle=f\Big(\frac{i_{1}}{n_{1}},\frac{i_{2}}{n_{2}}\Big)-2f\Big(\frac{i_{1}-1}{n_{1}},\frac{i_{2}}{n_{2}}\Big)+f\Big(\frac{i_{1}-2}{n_{1}},\frac{i_{2}}{n_{2}}\Big)=\theta_{i_{1},i_{2}}-2\theta_{i_{1}-1,i_{2}}+\theta_{i_{1}-2,i_{2}}

provided that
i_{1}\geq 2
.

If
\theta\in T
, then we can select
f
satisfying the required condition from
{\mathcal{F}^{d,s}_{\text{TC}}}
.
Let
f_{0}
denote such a function.
Since the divided difference of
f_{0}
of order
\mathbf{p}
is nonpositive for every
\mathbf{p}\in\{0,1,2\}^{d}
with
\max_{k}p_{k}=2
, it then follows that
(D^{(\mathbf{p})}\theta)_{\mathbf{i}}\leq 0
for all
\mathbf{p}\in\{0,1,2\}^{d}
with
\max_{k}p_{k}=2
and
\mathbf{i}=(i_{1},\dots,i_{d})\in I_{0}
with
i_{k}\geq p_{k}
for
k=1,\dots,d
.
Furthermore, using the interaction restriction condition (
13
) that
f_{0}
satisfies for every subset
S\subseteq[d]
with
|S|>s
, we can show that
(D^{(\mathbf{p})}\theta)_{\mathbf{i}}=0
for every
\mathbf{p}\in\{0,1\}^{d}
with
\sum_{k=1}^{d}p_{k}>s
and
\mathbf{i}=(i_{1},\dots,i_{d})\in I_{0}
with
i_{k}\geq p_{k}
for
k=1,\dots,d
.

Additionally, for a vector of positive integers
\mathbf{m}=(m_{1},\dots,m_{d})
, define

I(\mathbf{m})=\prod_{k=1}^{d}\{0,1,\dots,m_{k}-1\}.

(51)

Note that
I_{0}=I(n_{1},\dots,n_{d})
.
For
\theta\in{\mathbb{R}}^{|I(\mathbf{m})|}
and a vector of nonnegative integers
\mathbf{p}=(p_{1},\dots,p_{d})
, the discrete difference of
\theta
of order
\mathbf{p}
at
(i_{1},\dots,i_{d})
, where
(i_{1},\dots,i_{d})\in I(\mathbf{m})
and
i_{k}\geq p_{k}
for
k=1,\dots,d
, is defined analogously to (
50
).

Proof of Theorem
1
.

Let
h
be the function defined in (
48
) with
K=T
.
We aim to find
t_{2}>0
such that
h(t_{2})\leq 0
.
After that, we will apply Theorem
7
and Proposition
6
(with
K=T
) to derive an upper bound for the expected squared error
{\mathbb{E}}[\|\hat{\theta}_{T}-\theta^{*}\|_{2}^{2}]
.

To focus on the first term of
h(\cdot)
, we define

H(t)=h(t)+\frac{t^{2}}{2}={\mathbb{E}}\Big[\sup_{\theta\in T:\|\theta-\theta^{*}\|_{2}\leq t}\langle\xi,\theta-\theta^{*}\rangle\Big]

for
t\geq 0
.
For technical reasons, we decompose the index set
I_{0}
into
2^{d}
subsets with approximately equal size.
For each
k=1,\dots,d
, define

J_{0}^{(k)}=\Big\{i_{k}\in\mathbb{Z}:0\leq i_{k}\leq\frac{n_{k}}{2}-1\Big\}\ \text{ and }\ J_{1}^{(k)}=\Big\{i_{k}\in\mathbb{Z}:\frac{n_{k}-1}{2}\leq i_{k}\leq n_{k}-1\Big\}.

Also, for each
\delta\in\{0,1\}^{d}
, let

J_{\delta}=\prod_{k=1}^{d}J_{\delta_{k}}^{(k)}.

It is clear that
I_{0}=\biguplus_{\delta\in\{0,1\}^{d}}J_{\delta}
, where
\biguplus
denotes the disjoint union.
It thus follows that

H(t)={\mathbb{E}}\bigg[\sup_{\theta\in T:\|\theta-\theta^{*}\|_{2}\leq t}\sum_{\delta\in\{0,1\}^{d}}\sum_{\mathbf{i}\in J_{\delta}}\xi_{\mathbf{i}}(\theta_{\mathbf{i}}-\theta_{\mathbf{i}}^{*})\bigg]\leq\sum_{\delta\in\{0,1\}^{d}}{\mathbb{E}}\bigg[\sup_{\theta\in T:\|\theta-\theta^{*}\|_{2}\leq t}\sum_{\mathbf{i}\in J_{\delta}}\xi_{\mathbf{i}}(\theta_{\mathbf{i}}-\theta_{\mathbf{i}}^{*})\bigg]=\sum_{\delta\in\{0,1\}^{d}}H_{\delta}(t),

where

H_{\delta}(t)={\mathbb{E}}\bigg[\sup_{\theta\in T:\|\theta-\theta^{*}\|_{2}\leq t}\sum_{\mathbf{i}\in J_{\delta}}\xi_{\mathbf{i}}(\theta_{\mathbf{i}}-\theta_{\mathbf{i}}^{*})\bigg]

for each
\delta\in\{0,1\}^{d}
.

Here, we focus on bounding
H_{{\mathbf{0}}}(\cdot)
, as the bounds for the other
H_{\delta}(\cdot)
terms can be obtained analogously.
We further split the index set
J_{{\mathbf{0}}}
into dyadic pieces.
For each
k=1,\dots,d
, let
R_{k}
denote the largest integer
r_{k}
for which the set

L_{r_{k}}^{(k)}:=\Big\{i_{k}\in\mathbb{Z}:\frac{n_{k}}{2^{r_{k}+1}}-1<i_{k}\leq\frac{n_{k}}{2^{r_{k}}}-1\Big\}:=\big\{a_{r_{k}}^{(k)},a_{r_{k}}^{(k)}+1,\dots,b_{r_{k}}^{(k)}\big\}

is nonempty.
Observe that
L_{R_{k}}^{(k)}=\{0\}
and
2^{R_{k}}\leq n_{k}<2^{R_{k}+1}
for all
k=1,\dots,d
.
Define
\mathcal{R}=\prod_{k=1}^{d}\{1,\dots,R_{k}\}
.
Then,
J_{{\mathbf{0}}}
can be decomposed as follows:

J_{{\mathbf{0}}}=\biguplus_{\mathbf{r}\in\mathcal{R}}L_{\mathbf{r}},\quad\text{ where }\quad L_{\mathbf{r}}:=\prod_{k=1}^{d}L_{r_{k}}^{(k)}.

For
\theta=(\theta_{\mathbf{i}},\mathbf{i}\in I_{0})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{|I_{0}|}
and
\mathbf{r}=(r_{1},\dots,r_{d})\in\prod_{k=1}^{d}\{0,1,\dots,R_{k}\}
, let
\theta^{(\mathbf{r})}
denote the subvector of
\theta
consisting of the components whose indices belong to
L_{\mathbf{r}}
.

Now, define

W=\bigg\{(w_{\mathbf{r}},\mathbf{r}\in\mathcal{R}):w_{\mathbf{r}}\in\{1,\dots,|\mathcal{R}|\}\text{ for each }\mathbf{r}\in\mathcal{R}\text{ and }\sum_{\mathbf{r}\in\mathcal{R}}w_{\mathbf{r}}\leq 2|\mathcal{R}|\bigg\}.

Also, for each
\mathbf{w}=(w_{\mathbf{r}},\mathbf{r}\in\mathcal{R})\in W
, define

\Lambda_{\mathbf{w}}(t)=\Big\{\theta\in T:\|\theta-\theta^{*}\|_{2}\leq t\text{ and }\|\theta^{(\mathbf{r})}-{\theta^{*}}^{(\mathbf{r})}\|_{2}^{2}\leq\frac{w_{\mathbf{r}}t^{2}}{|\mathcal{R}|}\text{ for each }\mathbf{r}\in\mathcal{R}\Big\}.

We then claim that

\{\theta\in T:\|\theta-\theta^{*}\|_{2}\leq t\}\subseteq\bigcup_{\mathbf{w}\in W}\Lambda_{\mathbf{w}}(t).

(52)

This can be easily verified as follows.
Fix
\theta\in T
and assume that
\|\theta-\theta^{*}\|_{2}\leq t
.
For each
\mathbf{r}\in\mathcal{R}
, note that

\|\theta^{(\mathbf{r})}-{\theta^{*}}^{(\mathbf{r})}\|_{2}\leq\|\theta-\theta^{*}\|_{2}\leq t.

Thus, there exists
w_{\mathbf{r}}\in\{1,\dots,|\mathcal{R}|\}
such that

\frac{(w_{\mathbf{r}}-1)t^{2}}{|\mathcal{R}|}\leq\|\theta^{(\mathbf{r})}-{\theta^{*}}^{(\mathbf{r})}\|_{2}^{2}\leq\frac{w_{\mathbf{r}}t^{2}}{|\mathcal{R}|}.

Furthermore, since

t^{2}\geq\|\theta-\theta^{*}\|_{2}^{2}\geq\sum_{\mathbf{r}\in\mathcal{R}}\|\theta^{(\mathbf{r})}-{\theta^{*}}^{(\mathbf{r})}\|_{2}^{2}\geq\frac{t^{2}}{|\mathcal{R}|}\sum_{\mathbf{r}\in\mathcal{R}}(w_{\mathbf{r}}-1)=t^{2}\bigg(\frac{1}{|\mathcal{R}|}\sum_{\mathbf{r}\in\mathcal{R}}w_{\mathbf{r}}-1\bigg),

it follows that
\sum_{\mathbf{r}\in\mathcal{R}}w_{\mathbf{r}}\leq 2|\mathcal{R}|
.
Consequently,
\theta\in\Lambda_{\mathbf{w}}(t)
, completing the verification.

By (
52
), we have

H_{{\mathbf{0}}}(t)\leq{\mathbb{E}}\bigg[\max_{\mathbf{w}\in W}\sup_{\theta\in\Lambda_{\mathbf{w}}(t)}\sum_{\mathbf{i}\in J_{{\mathbf{0}}}}\xi_{\mathbf{i}}(\theta_{\mathbf{i}}-\theta_{\mathbf{i}}^{*})\bigg].

Using the following lemma, which is based on the concentration inequality for Gaussian random variables, we can switch the order of the expectation and maximum (at the cost of additional terms):

H_{{\mathbf{0}}}(t)\leq\max_{\mathbf{w}\in W}{\mathbb{E}}\bigg[\sup_{\theta\in\Lambda_{\mathbf{w}}(t)}\sum_{\mathbf{i}\in J_{{\mathbf{0}}}}\xi_{\mathbf{i}}(\theta_{\mathbf{i}}-\theta_{\mathbf{i}}^{*})\bigg]+\sigma t\sqrt{2\log|W|}+\sigma t\sqrt{\frac{\pi}{2}}.

Lemma 10

(Lemma D.1 of
Guntuboyina et al. [23]
)
.

Let
\Theta_{1},\dots,\Theta_{m}
be subsets of
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
containing the origin.
Then, we have

{\mathbb{E}}\Big[\max_{i=1,\dots,m}\sup_{\theta\in\Theta_{i}}\langle\xi,\theta\rangle\Big]\leq\max_{i=1,\dots,m}{\mathbb{E}}\Big[\sup_{\theta\in\Theta_{i}}\langle\xi,\theta\rangle\Big]+\sigma\Big(\sqrt{2\log m}+\sqrt{\frac{\pi}{2}}\Big)\cdot\Big(\max_{i=1,\dots,m}\sup_{\theta\in\Theta_{i}}\|\theta\|_{2}\Big),

where
\xi=(\xi_{1},\dots,\xi_{n})
is a vector of Gaussian errors with
\xi_{i}\overset{\text{i.i.d.}}{\sim}N(0,\sigma^{2})
.

Since

|W|\leq\sum_{l=|\mathcal{R}|}^{2|\mathcal{R}|}\binom{l-1}{l-|\mathcal{R}|}\leq\sum_{l=|\mathcal{R}|}^{2|\mathcal{R}|}\binom{2|\mathcal{R}|-1}{l-|\mathcal{R}|}\leq 2^{2|\mathcal{R}|-1},

it thus follows that

H_{{\mathbf{0}}}(t)\leq\max_{\mathbf{w}\in W}{\mathbb{E}}\bigg[\sup_{\theta\in\Lambda_{\mathbf{w}}(t)}\sum_{\mathbf{i}\in J_{{\mathbf{0}}}}\xi_{\mathbf{i}}(\theta_{\mathbf{i}}-\theta_{\mathbf{i}}^{*})\bigg]+2\sigma t\sqrt{|\mathcal{R}|}+\sigma t\sqrt{\frac{\pi}{2}}.

(53)

Fix
\mathbf{w}=(w_{\mathbf{r}},\mathbf{r}\in\mathcal{R})\in W
.
Since
J_{{\mathbf{0}}}=\biguplus_{\mathbf{r}\in\mathcal{R}}L_{\mathbf{r}}
, we have

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}\bigg[\sup_{\theta\in\Lambda_{\mathbf{w}}(t)}\sum_{\mathbf{i}\in J_{{\mathbf{0}}}}\xi_{\mathbf{i}}(\theta_{\mathbf{i}}-\theta_{\mathbf{i}}^{*})\bigg]={\mathbb{E}}\bigg[\sup_{\theta\in\Lambda_{\mathbf{w}}(t)}\sum_{\mathbf{r}\in\mathcal{R}}\langle\xi^{(\mathbf{r})},\theta^{(\mathbf{r})}-{\theta^{*}}^{(\mathbf{r})}\rangle\bigg]

\displaystyle\qquad\leq\sum_{\mathbf{r}\in\mathcal{R}}{\mathbb{E}}\bigg[\sup_{\theta\in\Lambda_{\mathbf{w}}(t)}\langle\xi^{(\mathbf{r})},\theta^{(\mathbf{r})}-{\theta^{*}}^{(\mathbf{r})}\rangle\bigg]\leq\sum_{\mathbf{r}\in\mathcal{R}}{\mathbb{E}}\bigg[\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\theta\in T:\|\theta-\theta^{*}\|_{2}\leq t\\
\|\theta^{(\mathbf{r})}-{\theta^{*}}^{(\mathbf{r})}\|_{2}\leq t(w_{\mathbf{r}}/|\mathcal{R}|)^{1/2}\end{subarray}}\langle\xi^{(\mathbf{r})},\theta^{(\mathbf{r})}-{\theta^{*}}^{(\mathbf{r})}\rangle\bigg].

For each
\mathbf{r}\in\mathcal{R}
, we now bound

{\mathbb{E}}\bigg[\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\theta\in T:\|\theta-\theta^{*}\|_{2}\leq t\\
\|\theta^{(\mathbf{r})}-{\theta^{*}}^{(\mathbf{r})}\|_{2}\leq t(w_{\mathbf{r}}/|\mathcal{R}|)^{1/2}\end{subarray}}\langle\xi^{(\mathbf{r})},\theta^{(\mathbf{r})}-{\theta^{*}}^{(\mathbf{r})}\rangle\bigg]

using the following result (Theorem
8
) established in the proof of Theorem 5.1 of
Ki et al. [33]
(an earlier version of
[
34
]
).

For a vector of positive integers
\mathbf{m}=(m_{1},\dots,m_{d})
and
\theta=(\theta_{\mathbf{i}},\mathbf{i}\in I(\mathbf{m}))
, define

V(\theta)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{p}\in\{0,1,2\}^{d}\\
\max_{j}p_{j}=2\end{subarray}}\bigg[\bigg(\prod_{k=1}^{d}m_{k}^{p_{k}-\mathbf{1}\{p_{k}=2\}}\bigg)\cdot\sum_{\mathbf{i}\in I^{(\mathbf{p})}(\mathbf{m})}|(D^{(\mathbf{p})}\theta)_{\mathbf{i}}|\bigg],

where
\mathbf{1}\{\cdot\}
denotes the indicator function.
Here, for each
\mathbf{p}\in\{0,1,2\}^{d}
with
\max_{j}p_{j}=2
, the index set
I^{(\mathbf{p})}(\mathbf{m})
is given by
I^{(\mathbf{p})}(\mathbf{m})=I^{(\mathbf{p})}_{1}\times\cdots\times I^{(\mathbf{p})}_{d}
, where

I^{(\mathbf{p})}_{k}=\begin{cases}\{p_{k}\}&\text{if }p_{k}\neq 2,\\
\{2,3,\dots,m_{k}-1\}&\text{if }p_{k}=2\end{cases}\qquad\text{for }k=1,\dots,d.

This
V(\cdot)
can be viewed as a discrete analogue of
V(\cdot)
defined in (
18
)
(see Section 9 of
Ki et al. [34]
).
Also, it is straightforward to verify that if
(D^{(\mathbf{p})}\theta)_{\mathbf{i}}\leq 0
for all
\mathbf{i}\in I^{(\mathbf{p})}(\mathbf{m})
and
\mathbf{p}\in\{0,1,2\}^{d}
with
\max_{j}p_{j}=2
(which holds when
\theta\in T
and
\mathbf{m}=(n_{1},\dots,n_{d})
), then
V(\theta)
can be expressed as

V(\theta)=\sum_{\mathbf{q}\in\{0,1\}^{d}\setminus\{{\mathbf{0}}\}}\bigg[\bigg(\prod_{k\in S_{\mathbf{q}}}m_{k}\bigg)\cdot\Big((D^{(\mathbf{q})}\theta)_{(1,k\in S_{\mathbf{q}})\times(0,k\notin S_{\mathbf{q}})}-(D^{(\mathbf{q})}\theta)_{(m_{k}-1,k\in S_{\mathbf{q}})\times(0,k\notin S_{\mathbf{q}})}\Big)\bigg],

(54)

where
S_{\mathbf{q}}:=\{k\in\{1,\dots,d\}:q_{k}=1\}
.
Here, for each
\mathbf{q}
,
(1,k\in S_{\mathbf{q}})\times(0,k\notin S_{\mathbf{q}})
represents the
d
-dimensional vector whose
k^{\text{th}}
component is
1
if
k\in S_{\mathbf{q}}
, 0 otherwise;
(m_{k}-1,k\in S_{\mathbf{q}})\times(0,k\notin S_{\mathbf{q}})
is similarly defined.
Moreover, for each
V>0
, define

\displaystyle C_{\mathbf{m}}^{d,s}(V)

\displaystyle=\bigg\{\theta\in{\mathbb{R}}^{|I(\mathbf{m})|}:V(\theta)\leq V\text{ and }(D^{(\mathbf{p})}\theta)_{\mathbf{i}}=0\text{ for every }\mathbf{p}\in\{0,1\}^{d}\text{ with }\sum_{k=1}^{d}p_{k}>s

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\text{ and }\mathbf{i}\in I(\mathbf{m})\text{ with }i_{k}\geq p_{k}\text{ for }k=1,\dots,d\bigg\}.

Theorem 8
.

There exists some constant
C_{d}
depending only on
d
such that, for every
t>0
,

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}\Big[\sup_{\theta\in C_{\mathbf{m}}^{d,s}(V):\|\theta-\theta^{*}\|_{2}\leq t}\langle\xi,\theta-\theta^{*}\rangle\Big]

\displaystyle\leq C_{d}\sigma t\log(m_{1}\cdots m_{d})+C_{d}\sigma t\log\Big(1+\frac{V}{t}\Big)

\displaystyle\quad+C_{d}\sigma t\bigg[\log\Big(2+\frac{V(m_{1}\cdots m_{d})^{1/2}}{t}\Big)\bigg]^{3(2s-1)/8}

\displaystyle\quad+C_{d}\sigma V^{1/4}t^{3/4}(m_{1}\cdots m_{d})^{1/8}\bigg[\log\Big(2+\frac{V(m_{1}\cdots m_{d})^{1/2}}{t}\Big)\bigg]^{3(2s-1)/8}

when
s\geq 2
, and

{\mathbb{E}}\Big[\sup_{\theta\in C_{\mathbf{m}}^{d,1}(V):\|\theta-\theta^{*}\|_{2}\leq t}\langle\xi,\theta-\theta^{*}\rangle\Big]\leq C_{d}\sigma t\log(m_{1}\cdots m_{d})+C_{d}\sigma t\log\Big(1+\frac{V}{t}\Big)+C_{d}\sigma V^{1/4}t^{3/4}(m_{1}\cdots m_{d})^{1/8}

when
s=1
.

We will apply this theorem to the subvectors
\theta^{(\mathbf{r})}
.
For each
\mathbf{r}\in\mathcal{R}
, define

m_{r_{k}}^{(k)}:=|L_{r_{k}}^{(k)}|=b_{r_{k}}^{(k)}-a_{r_{k}}^{(k)}+1\qquad\text{ for }k=1,\dots,d,

and let
\mathbf{m}_{\mathbf{r}}=(m_{r_{1}}^{(1)},\dots,m_{r_{d}}^{(d)})
.
We re-index each
\theta^{(\mathbf{\mathbf{r}})}
with
I(\mathbf{m}_{\mathbf{r}})
by shifting indices, ensuring that the definition of
V(\cdot)
is applicable to
\theta^{(\mathbf{r})}
.
We will show that, for each
\mathbf{r}\in\mathcal{R}
, there exists some
V_{\mathbf{r}}>0
such that
\theta^{(\mathbf{r})}\in C_{\mathbf{m}_{\mathbf{r}}}^{d,s}(V_{\mathbf{r}})
for every
\theta\in T
satisfying
\|\theta-\theta^{*}\|_{2}\leq t
.

Fix
\theta\in T
and assume that
\|\theta-\theta^{*}\|_{2}\leq t
.
Since
\theta\in T
, it follows that
(D^{(\mathbf{p})}\theta)_{\mathbf{i}}=0
for every
\mathbf{p}\in\{0,1\}^{d}
with
\sum_{k=1}^{d}p_{k}>s
and
\mathbf{i}=(i_{1},\dots,i_{d})\in I_{0}
with
i_{k}\geq p_{k}
for
k=1,\dots,d
.
Thus, for every
\mathbf{p}\in\{0,1\}^{d}
with
\sum_{k=1}^{d}p_{k}>s
and
\mathbf{i}=(i_{1},\dots,i_{d})\in I(\mathbf{m}_{\mathbf{r}})
with
i_{k}\geq p_{k}
for
k=1,\dots,d
, we have

(D^{(\mathbf{p})}\theta^{(\mathbf{r})})_{\mathbf{i}}=(D^{(\mathbf{p})}\theta)_{i_{1}+a_{r_{1}}^{(1)},\dots,i_{d}+a_{r_{d}}^{(d)}}=0.

Next, we bound
V(\theta^{(\mathbf{r})})
for each
\mathbf{r}\in\mathcal{R}
.
To this end, we use the following lemma, which will be proved in Appendix
C.5.1
.
Choose any function
g^{*}\in{\mathcal{F}^{d,s}_{\text{TC}}}
that agrees with
f^{*}
at the design points
\mathbf{x}^{(\mathbf{i})}
,
and define

\begin{split}M:=M(g^{*}):=\max_{S:1\leq|S|\leq s}\max\bigg(&\sup\bigg\{\begin{bmatrix}0,t_{k},&k\in S&\hbox{\multirowsetup; \ $g^{*}$ }\\
0,&k\notin S&\end{bmatrix}:t_{k}>0\ \text{ for }k\in S\bigg\},\\
&\quad-\inf\bigg\{\begin{bmatrix}t_{k},1,&k\in S&\hbox{\multirowsetup; \ $g^{*}$ }\\
0,&k\notin S&\end{bmatrix}:t_{k}<1\ \text{ for }k\in S\bigg\}\bigg).\end{split}

(55)

Since
g^{*}\in{\mathcal{F}^{d,s}_{\text{TC}}}
, it holds that
M(g^{*})<+\infty
.
Also, note that
\theta^{*}=(f^{*}({\mathbf{x}}^{(\mathbf{i})}),\mathbf{i}\in I_{0})=(g^{*}({\mathbf{x}}^{(\mathbf{i})}),\mathbf{i}\in I_{0})
.

Lemma 11
.

Assume that
\theta\in T
and
\|\theta-\theta^{*}\|_{2}\leq t
.
Then, for every
\mathbf{p}=(p_{1},\dots,p_{d})\in\{0,1\}^{d}\setminus\{{\mathbf{0}}\}
and
\mathbf{i}=(i_{1},\dots,i_{d})\in L_{r}
with
i_{k}\geq p_{k}
for
k=1,\dots,d
, we have

(D^{(\mathbf{p})}\theta)_{\mathbf{i}}\geq\frac{C_{d}}{\prod_{k=1}^{d}n_{k}^{p_{k}}}\cdot\Big[-M-t\Big(\frac{2^{r_{+}}}{n}\Big)^{1/2}\cdot 2^{\sum_{k=1}^{d}p_{k}r_{k}}\Big]

(56)

if
\mathbf{r}=(r_{1},\dots,r_{d})\in\prod_{k=1}^{d}\{1,\dots,R_{k}\}=\mathcal{R}
, and

(D^{(\mathbf{p})}\theta)_{\mathbf{i}}\leq\frac{C_{d}}{\prod_{k=1}^{d}n_{k}^{p_{k}}}\cdot\Big[M+t\Big(\frac{2^{r_{+}}}{n}\Big)^{1/2}\cdot 2^{\sum_{k=1}^{d}p_{k}r_{k}}\Big]

(57)

if
\mathbf{r}=(r_{1},\dots,r_{d})\in\prod_{k=1}^{d}\{0,1,\dots,R_{k}\}
, where
r_{+}=\sum_{k=1}^{d}r_{k}
.

Recall that
S_{\mathbf{p}}=\{k\in\{1,\dots,d\}:p_{k}=1\}
for each
\mathbf{p}\in\{0,1\}^{d}
.
By Lemma
11
and (
54
), for each
\mathbf{r}=(r_{1},\dots,r_{d})\in\mathcal{R}
, we have

\displaystyle V(\theta^{(\mathbf{r})})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathbf{p}\in\{0,1\}^{d}\setminus\{{\mathbf{0}}\}}\prod_{k\in S_{\mathbf{p}}}m_{r_{k}}^{(k)}\cdot\Big[(D^{(\mathbf{p})}\theta^{(\mathbf{r})})_{(1,k\in S_{\mathbf{p}})\times(0,k\notin S_{\mathbf{p}})}-(D^{(\mathbf{p})}\theta^{(\mathbf{r})})_{(m^{(k)}_{r_{k}}-1,k\in S_{\mathbf{p}})\times(0,k\notin S_{\mathbf{p}})}\Big]

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathbf{p}\in\{0,1\}^{d}\setminus\{{\mathbf{0}}\}}\prod_{k\in S_{\mathbf{p}}}m_{r_{k}}^{(k)}\cdot\Big[(D^{(\mathbf{p})}\theta)_{(a_{r_{k}}^{(k)}+1,k\in S_{\mathbf{p}})\times(a_{r_{k}}^{(k)},k\notin S_{\mathbf{p}})}-(D^{(\mathbf{p})}\theta)_{(b_{r_{k}}^{(k)},k\in S_{\mathbf{p}})\times(a_{r_{k}}^{(k)},k\notin S_{\mathbf{p}})}\Big]

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{\mathbf{p}\in\{0,1\}^{d}\setminus\{{\mathbf{0}}\}}\prod_{k=1}^{d}\Big(\frac{n_{k}}{2^{r_{k}}}\Big)^{p_{k}}\cdot\frac{C_{d}}{\prod_{k=1}^{d}n_{k}^{p_{k}}}\cdot\Big[M+t\Big(\frac{2^{r_{+}}}{n}\Big)^{1/2}\cdot 2^{\sum_{k=1}^{d}p_{k}r_{k}}\Big]

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{\mathbf{p}\in\{0,1\}^{d}\setminus\{{\mathbf{0}}\}}C_{d}\cdot\Big[\frac{M}{2^{\sum_{k=1}^{d}p_{k}r_{k}}}+t\Big(\frac{2^{r_{+}}}{n}\Big)^{1/2}\Big]\leq C_{d}\cdot\Big[M+t\Big(\frac{2^{r_{+}}}{n}\Big)^{1/2}\Big]:=V_{\mathbf{r}}.

Here, for the first inequality, we use

m_{r_{k}}^{(k)}=b_{r_{k}}^{(k)}-a_{r_{k}}^{(k)}+1\leq\frac{n_{k}}{2^{r_{k}}}-1+1=\frac{n_{k}}{2^{r_{k}}}

for
k=1,\dots,d
.
Thus, we have
\theta^{(\mathbf{r})}\in C_{\mathbf{m}_{\mathbf{r}}}^{d,s}(V_{\mathbf{r}})
for each
\mathbf{r}\in\mathcal{R}
.

From now on, we handle the two cases
s\geq 2
and
s=1
separately.
Let us first consider the case
s\geq 2
.
By Theorem
8
,
for each
\mathbf{r}=(r_{1},\dots,r_{d})\in\mathcal{R}
, we have

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}\bigg[\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\theta\in T:\|\theta-\theta^{*}\|_{2}\leq t\\
\|\theta^{(\mathbf{r})}-{\theta^{*}}^{(\mathbf{r})}\|_{2}\leq t(w_{\mathbf{r}}/|\mathcal{R}|)^{1/2}\end{subarray}}\langle\xi^{(\mathbf{r})},\theta^{(\mathbf{r})}-{\theta^{*}}^{(\mathbf{r})}\rangle\bigg]\leq{\mathbb{E}}\bigg[\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\theta^{(\mathbf{r})}\in C_{\mathbf{m}}^{d,s}(V_{\mathbf{r}})\\
\|\theta^{(\mathbf{r})}-{\theta^{*}}^{(\mathbf{r})}\|_{2}\leq t_{\mathbf{r}}\end{subarray}}\langle\xi^{(\mathbf{r})},\theta^{(\mathbf{r})}-{\theta^{*}}^{(\mathbf{r})}\rangle\bigg]

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\qquad\leq C_{d}\sigma t_{\mathbf{r}}\log|L_{\mathbf{r}}|+C_{d}\sigma t_{\mathbf{r}}\log\Big(1+\frac{V_{\mathbf{r}}}{t_{\mathbf{r}}}\Big)+C_{d}\sigma t_{\mathbf{r}}\bigg[\log\Big(2+\frac{V_{\mathbf{r}}|L_{\mathbf{r}}|^{1/2}}{t_{\mathbf{r}}}\Big)\bigg]^{3(2s-1)/8}\\
&\qquad\qquad+C_{d}\sigma V_{\mathbf{r}}^{1/4}t_{\mathbf{r}}^{3/4}|L_{\mathbf{r}}|^{1/8}\bigg[\log\Big(2+\frac{V_{\mathbf{r}}|L_{\mathbf{r}}|^{1/2}}{t_{\mathbf{r}}}\Big)\bigg]^{3(2s-1)/8},\end{split}

(58)

where
t_{\mathbf{r}}=t(w_{\mathbf{r}}/|\mathcal{R}|)^{1/2}
.
Recall that

{\mathbb{E}}\bigg[\sup_{\theta\in\Lambda_{\mathbf{w}}(t)}\sum_{\mathbf{i}\in J_{{\mathbf{0}}}}\xi_{\mathbf{i}}(\theta_{\mathbf{i}}-\theta_{\mathbf{i}}^{*})\bigg]\leq\sum_{\mathbf{r}\in\mathcal{R}}{\mathbb{E}}\bigg[\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\theta\in T:\|\theta-\theta^{*}\|_{2}\leq t\\
\|\theta^{(\mathbf{r})}-{\theta^{*}}^{(\mathbf{r})}\|_{2}\leq t(w_{\mathbf{r}}/|\mathcal{R}|)^{1/2}\end{subarray}}\langle\xi^{(\mathbf{r})},\theta^{(\mathbf{r})}-{\theta^{*}}^{(\mathbf{r})}\rangle\bigg].

Hence, we need to bound the summation of each term in
(
58
)
over
\mathcal{R}
.
The summation of the first term over
\mathbf{r}\in\mathcal{R}
can be bounded as

\displaystyle\sum_{\mathbf{r}\in\mathcal{R}}t_{\mathbf{r}}\log|L_{\mathbf{r}}|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{\mathbf{r}\in\mathcal{R}}t\cdot\Big(\frac{w_{\mathbf{r}}}{|\mathcal{R}|}\Big)^{1/2}\log\Big(\prod_{k=1}^{d}\frac{n_{k}}{2^{r_{k}}}\Big)\leq\frac{t\log n}{|\mathcal{R}|^{1/2}}\cdot\sum_{\mathbf{r}\in\mathcal{R}}w_{\mathbf{r}}^{1/2}\leq t\log n\cdot\bigg(\sum_{\mathbf{r}\in\mathcal{R}}w_{\mathbf{r}}\bigg)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\leq t\log n\cdot(2|\mathcal{R}|)^{1/2}\leq C_{d}t(\log n)^{(d+2)/2}.

Here, the first inequality follows from the fact that

|L_{\mathbf{r}}|=\prod_{k=1}^{d}|L_{r_{k}}^{(k)}|=\prod_{k=1}^{d}m_{r_{k}}^{(k)}\leq\prod_{k=1}^{d}\frac{n_{k}}{2^{r_{k}}}.

Also, the second-to-last inequality is due to the condition
\sum_{\mathbf{r}\in\mathcal{R}}w_{\mathbf{r}}\leq 2|\mathcal{R}|
, and the last one follows from

|\mathcal{R}|=\prod_{k=1}^{d}R_{k}\leq\prod_{k=1}^{d}(1+C\log n_{k})\leq\bigg[\frac{1}{d}\sum_{k=1}^{d}(1+C\log n_{k})\bigg]^{d}=\Big(1+\frac{C}{d}\cdot\log n\Big)^{d}\leq C_{d}(\log n)^{d}.

We can bound the the summation of the second term over
\mathbf{r}\in\mathcal{R}
as follows:

\displaystyle\sum_{\mathbf{r}\in\mathcal{R}}t_{\mathbf{r}}\log\Big(1+\frac{V_{\mathbf{r}}}{t_{\mathbf{r}}}\Big)=\sum_{\mathbf{r}\in\mathcal{R}}t\Big(\frac{w_{\mathbf{r}}}{|\mathcal{R}|}\Big)^{1/2}\cdot\log\Big(1+C_{d}\cdot\frac{M+t(2^{r_{+}}/n)^{1/2}}{t\cdot(w_{\mathbf{r}}/|\mathcal{R}|)^{1/2}}\Big)

\displaystyle\qquad\leq\sum_{\mathbf{r}\in\mathcal{R}}t\Big(\frac{w_{\mathbf{r}}}{|\mathcal{R}|}\Big)^{1/2}\cdot\log\Big(1+C_{d}\cdot\frac{(M+t)\cdot|\mathcal{R}|^{1/2}}{t}\Big)\leq C_{d}t(\log n)^{d/2}\cdot\log\Big(1+C_{d}\cdot\frac{(M+t)\cdot(\log n)^{d/2}}{t}\Big)

\displaystyle\qquad\leq C_{d}t(\log n)^{d/2}\cdot\Big[C_{d}+\log\Big(1+\frac{M}{t}\Big)+\frac{d}{2}\log\log n\Big]

\displaystyle\qquad\leq C_{d}t(\log n)^{d/2}+C_{d}t(\log n)^{d/2}\log\Big(1+\frac{M}{t}\Big)+C_{d}t(\log n)^{d/2}\cdot\log\log n,

where the first inequality follows from that
2^{r_{+}}\leq n
and
w_{\mathbf{r}}\geq 1
.
Similarly, the summation of the third term over
\mathbf{r}\in\mathcal{R}
can be bounded as

\displaystyle\sum_{\mathbf{r}\in\mathcal{R}}t_{\mathbf{r}}\bigg[\log\Big(2+\frac{V_{\mathbf{r}}|L_{\mathbf{r}}|^{1/2}}{t_{\mathbf{r}}}\Big)\bigg]^{3(2s-1)/8}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{\mathbf{r}\in\mathcal{R}}t\cdot\Big(\frac{w_{\mathbf{r}}}{|\mathcal{R}|}\Big)^{1/2}\bigg[\log\Big(2+C_{d}\cdot\frac{M+t(2^{r_{+}}/n)^{1/2}}{t\cdot(w_{\mathbf{r}}/|\mathcal{R}|)^{1/2}}\cdot n^{1/2}\Big)\bigg]^{3(2s-1)/8}

\displaystyle\leq C_{d}t(\log n)^{d/2}\cdot\bigg[\log\Big(2+C_{d}\cdot\frac{(M+t)\cdot n^{1/2}(\log n)^{d/2}}{t}\Big)\bigg]^{3(2s-1)/8}

\displaystyle\leq C_{d}t(\log n)^{d/2}\cdot\Big[C_{d}+\log\Big(1+\frac{M}{t}\Big)+\frac{1}{2}\log n+\frac{d}{2}\log\log n\Big]^{3(2s-1)/8}

\displaystyle\leq C_{d}t(\log n)^{d/2}\Big[\log\Big(1+\frac{M}{t}\Big)\Big]^{3(2s-1)/8}+C_{d}t(\log n)^{(4d+6s-3)/8},

where the first inequality uses the fact that
|L_{\mathbf{r}}|\leq n
.
Lastly, the summation of the fourth term over
\mathbf{r}\in\mathcal{R}
has the following bound:

\displaystyle\sum_{\mathbf{r}\in\mathcal{R}}V_{\mathbf{r}}^{1/4}t_{\mathbf{r}}^{3/4}|L_{\mathbf{r}}|^{1/8}\bigg[\log\Big(2+\frac{V_{\mathbf{r}}|L_{\mathbf{r}}|^{1/2}}{t_{\mathbf{r}}}\Big)\bigg]^{3(2s-1)/8}

\displaystyle\qquad\leq\sum_{\mathbf{r}\in\mathcal{R}}C_{d}\Big[M+t\Big(\frac{2^{r_{+}}}{n}\Big)^{1/2}\Big]^{1/4}\cdot\Big(t\Big(\frac{w_{\mathbf{r}}}{|\mathcal{R}|}\Big)^{1/2}\Big)^{3/4}\cdot\bigg(\prod_{k=1}^{d}\frac{n_{k}}{2^{r_{k}-1}}\bigg)^{1/8}\Big[\log\Big(1+\frac{M}{t}\Big)+\log n\Big]^{3(2s-1)/8}

\displaystyle\qquad\leq C_{d}\sum_{\mathbf{r}\in\mathcal{R}}\Big[M^{1/4}+t^{1/4}\Big(\frac{2^{r_{+}}}{n}\Big)^{1/8}\Big]\cdot t^{3/4}\Big(\frac{w_{\mathbf{r}}}{|\mathcal{R}|}\Big)^{3/8}\cdot\Big(\frac{n^{1/8}}{2^{r_{+}/8}}\Big)\cdot\Big[\log\Big(1+\frac{M}{t}\Big)+\log n\Big]^{3(2s-1)/8}

\displaystyle\qquad\leq C_{d}\bigg[M^{1/4}t^{3/4}n^{1/8}\sum_{\mathbf{r}\in\mathcal{R}}\frac{1}{2^{r_{+}/8}}+t\sum_{\mathbf{r}\in\mathcal{R}}\Big(\frac{w_{\mathbf{r}}}{|\mathcal{R}|}\Big)^{3/8}\bigg]\cdot\Big[\log\Big(1+\frac{M}{t}\Big)+\log n\Big]^{3(2s-1)/8}.

Here, the second inequality is based on that
(x+y)^{1/4}\leq x^{1/4}+y^{1/4}
for
x,y\geq 0
.
Observe that

\sum_{\mathbf{r}\in\mathcal{R}}\frac{1}{2^{r_{+}/8}}=\prod_{k=1}^{d}\sum_{r_{k}=1}^{R_{k}}\frac{1}{2^{r_{k}/8}}\leq C.

Also, by Hölder inequality, we have

\sum_{\mathbf{r}\in\mathcal{R}}\Big(\frac{w_{\mathbf{r}}}{|\mathcal{R}|}\Big)^{3/8}\leq\frac{1}{|\mathcal{R}|^{3/8}}\cdot\bigg(\sum_{\mathbf{r}\in\mathcal{R}}w_{\mathbf{r}}\bigg)^{3/8}\cdot\bigg(\sum_{\mathbf{r}\in\mathcal{R}}1\bigg)^{5/8}\leq C|\mathcal{R}|^{5/8}\leq C_{d}(\log n)^{5d/8}.

It thus follows that

\displaystyle\sum_{\mathbf{r}\in\mathcal{R}}V_{\mathbf{r}}^{1/4}t_{\mathbf{r}}^{3/4}|L_{\mathbf{r}}|^{1/8}\bigg[\log\Big(2+\frac{V_{\mathbf{r}}|L_{\mathbf{r}}|^{1/2}}{t_{\mathbf{r}}}\Big)\bigg]^{3(2s-1)/8}\leq C_{d}M^{1/4}t^{3/4}n^{1/8}\Big[\log\Big(1+\frac{M}{t}\Big)\Big]^{3(2s-1)/8}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+C_{d}M^{1/4}t^{3/4}n^{1/8}(\log n)^{3(2s-1)/8}+C_{d}t(\log n)^{5d/8}\Big[\log\Big(1+\frac{M}{t}\Big)\Big]^{3(2s-1)/8}+C_{d}t(\log n)^{(5d+6s-3)/8}.

Combining these results, we can derive that

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}\bigg[\sup_{\theta\in\Lambda_{\mathbf{w}}(t)}\sum_{\mathbf{i}\in J_{{\mathbf{0}}}}\xi_{\mathbf{i}}(\theta_{\mathbf{i}}-\theta_{\mathbf{i}}^{*})\bigg]\leq C_{d}\sigma t(\log n)^{\max((d+2)/2,(5d+6s-3)/8)}+C_{d}\sigma t(\log n)^{d/2}\log\Big(1+\frac{M}{t}\Big)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\quad+C_{d}\sigma t(\log n)^{5d/8}\Big[\log\Big(1+\frac{M}{t}\Big)\Big]^{3(2s-1)/8}+C_{d}\sigma M^{1/4}t^{3/4}n^{1/8}\Big[\log\Big(1+\frac{M}{t}\Big)\Big]^{3(2s-1)/8}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\quad+C_{d}\sigma M^{1/4}t^{3/4}n^{1/8}(\log n)^{3(2s-1)/8}.

Using (
53
), we can show that
H_{{\mathbf{0}}}(t)
has the same upper bound (with a slightly larger
C_{d}
).
Similarly, by analogous arguments, we can derive the same upper bound for
H_{\delta}(t)
for
\delta\in\{0,1\}^{d}\setminus\{{\mathbf{0}}\}
.
Therefore, we can see that if we define

\displaystyle t_{2}

\displaystyle=\max\bigg(\sigma,C_{d}\sigma(\log n)^{\max((d+2)/2,(5d+6s-3)/8)},C_{d}\sigma(\log n)^{d/2}\cdot\log\Big(1+\frac{M}{\sigma}\Big),

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\quad C_{d}\sigma(\log n)^{5d/8}\Big[\log\Big(1+\frac{M}{\sigma}\Big)\Big]^{3(2s-1)/8},C_{d}\sigma^{4/5}M^{1/5}n^{1/10}\Big[\log\Big(1+\frac{M}{\sigma}\Big)\Big]^{3(2s-1)/10},

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\quad C_{d}\sigma^{4/5}M^{1/5}n^{1/10}(\log n)^{3(2s-1)/10}\bigg),

then
H(t_{2})\leq t_{2}^{2}/2
, which implies
h(t_{2})\leq 0
.
As a result, by Theorem
7
and Proposition
6
, we have

\displaystyle R_{F}(\hat{f}_{\text{TC}}^{d,s},f^{*})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\cdot{\mathbb{E}}\big[\|\hat{\theta}_{T}-\theta^{*}\|_{2}^{2}\big]\leq\frac{C}{n}\cdot(\sigma^{2}+t_{2}^{2})

\displaystyle\leq C_{d}\Big(\frac{\sigma^{2}M^{1/2}}{n}\Big)^{4/5}(\log n)^{3(2s-1)/5}+C_{d}\Big(\frac{\sigma^{2}M^{1/2}}{n}\Big)^{4/5}\Big[\log\Big(1+\frac{M}{\sigma}\Big)\Big]^{3(2s-1)/5}

\displaystyle\qquad+C_{d}\cdot\frac{\sigma^{2}}{n}(\log n)^{5d/4}\Big[\log\Big(1+\frac{M}{\sigma}\Big)\Big]^{3(2s-1)/4}+C_{d}\cdot\frac{\sigma^{2}}{n}(\log n)^{d}\Big[\log\Big(1+\frac{M}{\sigma}\Big)\Big]^{2}

\displaystyle\qquad+C_{d}\cdot\frac{\sigma^{2}}{n}(\log n)^{\max(d+2,(5d+6s-3)/4)}.

This can be simplified as

R_{F}(\hat{f}_{\text{TC}}^{d,s},f^{*})\leq C_{d}\Big(\frac{\sigma^{2}M^{1/2}}{n}\Big)^{4/5}\bigg[\log\Big(\Big(1+\frac{M}{\sigma}\Big)n\Big)\bigg]^{3(2s-1)/5}+C_{d}\cdot\frac{\sigma^{2}}{n}(\log n)^{5d/4}\bigg[\log\Big(\Big(1+\frac{M}{\sigma}\Big)n\Big)\bigg]^{3(2s-1)/4},

(59)

as desired, since
s\geq 2
.

We now consider the case
s=1
.
In this case, again, by Theorem
8
, we have

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}\bigg[\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\theta\in T:\|\theta-\theta^{*}\|_{2}\leq t\\
\|\theta^{(\mathbf{r})}-{\theta^{*}}^{(\mathbf{r})}\|_{2}\leq t(w_{\mathbf{r}}/|\mathcal{R}|)^{1/2}\end{subarray}}\langle\xi^{(\mathbf{r})},\theta^{(\mathbf{r})}-{\theta^{*}}^{(\mathbf{r})}\rangle\bigg]\leq{\mathbb{E}}\bigg[\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\theta^{(\mathbf{r})}\in C_{\mathbf{m}}^{d,s}(V_{\mathbf{r}})\\
\|\theta^{(\mathbf{r})}-{\theta^{*}}^{(\mathbf{r})}\|_{2}\leq t_{\mathbf{r}}\end{subarray}}\langle\xi^{(\mathbf{r})},\theta^{(\mathbf{r})}-{\theta^{*}}^{(\mathbf{r})}\rangle\bigg]

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\leq C_{d}\sigma t_{\mathbf{r}}\log|L_{\mathbf{r}}|+C_{d}\sigma t_{\mathbf{r}}\log\Big(1+\frac{V_{\mathbf{r}}}{t_{\mathbf{r}}}\Big)+C_{d}\sigma V_{\mathbf{r}}^{1/4}t_{\mathbf{r}}^{3/4}|L_{\mathbf{r}}|^{1/8}.

Through similar computations as above, we can show that
